# Engineering Lessons — HalaTuju

- **Where a row LIVES is not whose work it represents — and when the two disagree, the navigation usually got it right first.** I argued that sponsor emails might belong on the platform row because `Sponsor` is a platform-level table with no organisation FK. True, and irrelevant: it is platform-level so one person can hold one login across organisations, an IDENTITY decision. The owner answered from the product instead — sponsors sit under ORGANISATION in the sidebar, and you invite one from the organisation's own Invitations page — and the nav registry agreed with them in code (`scope: 'organisation'`) long before the argument. **A data model answers "what is this thing attached to"; the IA answers "whose activity is this", and billing is a question of the second kind.** When a scoping decision feels genuinely balanced, check what the console already tells the user it is: that classification was made deliberately, by someone thinking about the reader rather than the schema. (Billing attribution, 2026-08-19)
- **"Fixed at the seams that fire" fixes the seams that fire — and every seam added afterwards is born broken.** Billing attribution threads the tenant through an ambient `usage_context` the CALLER opens, with no default and nothing checking it. The July pass wrapped the four email senders live at the time and recorded the job as done; it was, for those four. Three weeks later 125 of August's emails sat on the platform row, because eight more senders had started firing and none of them knew they had to opt in. **The tell is a fix whose correctness depends on every future caller remembering something** — that is not a fix, it is a convention, and a convention with a silent failure mode decays at the rate new callers are written. The symptom is the worst kind: not an error, but a plausible-looking line in a bucket whose label ("shared platform base") explains it away. When you catch yourself writing "wrapped the N call sites that exist", the deliverable is the N+1th — make the omission impossible, or make it loud. (Billing attribution, 2026-08-18)
- **A card that is EMPTY is not merely useless — it is usually asserting something, and on a case that has ended the assertion is usually false.** Three boxes survived the closed-case sweep because each renders read-only text and looked harmless. Read as sentences they were not: a profile card titling itself "(draft)" with no draft, promising a final version "when you save your verdict" at an endpoint that now refuses one, and explaining a handoff that will never occur; a Check 2 box reporting "all student tasks are clear" about a student nobody ever asked for anything; and a lock line naming an interview that never happened. **The empty state is where stale copy hides**, because it is written once, early, for the happy path, and no test asserts prose. Worth a pass of its own on any surface with a terminal state: render each card with nothing in it, read the words aloud, and ask which tense they are in — a future tense on a closed record is always a bug. (Closed-case cockpit follow-up, 2026-08-18)
- **A rule written for ONE end of a lifecycle is silently absent at the other, and the codebase will already contain the list it needed.** `showsPostSubmissionCards` is `status !== 'shortlisted'` — correct, tested, owner-decided, and blind to every terminal status, so a case that expired or was rejected before review displayed the full review UI with live controls. What makes this worth writing down is that the answer was already sitting two functions away: `QUERYING_LOCKED_STATES` and `REVIEWER_CARD_HIDDEN_FROM` both list the same three off-ramps, and Check 2 was correctly reading *"Querying closed"* two boxes above the box inviting a reviewer to type. **When a gate is defined as "not X", ask what it says about Y** — and grep the module for an existing set naming Y before writing a new one; a second list that means the same thing is the next drift. (Closed-case cockpit, 2026-08-18)
- **"The endpoint refuses it anyway" is worth checking rather than assuming — and when two endpoints run in sequence, the gate on the SECOND one protects nothing the first has already written.** The cockpit's Approve path calls `record-verdict` and then `verify-accept`. Only the second has a status gate, so on a rejected application the first would have stamped a verdict and an award amount before the second returned 400 — the visible outcome being an error message, with the write already committed. The 2026-07-30 sprint had fixed exactly this "a rejected application must not hold an award amount" from the decline side; nobody thought to ask which OTHER paths reach the same field. **For any multi-call action, the effective gate is the gate on the FIRST call that writes** — read the sequence, not the endpoint you happened to open. (Closed-case cockpit, 2026-08-18)
- **Before hiding a dead control, ask whether the same box is also somebody's RECORD.** The instinct on a rejected case was to hide the Recommendation card outright. Half the rejected records on production carry a recorded verdict, and that card is where their *"Declined by … · date"* trail renders — a status-only rule would have removed the audit trail from every one of them while fixing a cosmetic problem on the other half. The gate had to read a second fact (is there a verdict? is there an interview?) rather than status alone. **A card is a control when it is empty and a record when it is full**; the query that settles it is "how many production rows are in the FULL state", and it took one line of SQL. (Closed-case cockpit, 2026-08-18)

- **Twice in one day, a rule scoped by its CONTAINER failed when a row's audience changed — scope rules by what they PROTECT.** Morning: the tax-relief ban lived in the sponsor email family, so the organisation's sponsor invitation — a donor pitch implemented as a `PartnerEmailTemplate` — was validated by the partner list, which never banned it. Afternoon: a guard forbidding any email from pointing at a console excluded the reviewer kinds BY TABLE, so the new admin joining letter tripped it despite its reader having a console; its own docstring already stated the correct principle ("a surface that does not exist for the reader"), and the implementation had encoded the table instead. Both were invisible from inside either module, because each looked internally complete. **The question that finds them: for this row, which guard actually runs — and does that guard know who the reader is?** Ask it whenever content moves into an existing family, and prefer a shared home for rules that are true-or-false about the world (a legal claim, a safety statement) over rules that genuinely vary by audience (tone, vocabulary). (Invitation emails, 2026-08-04)

- **A "never overwrite what somebody may have edited" rule is also a ONE-WAY VALVE: your rewrite of the built-in reaches nobody once the row exists.** The seed command creates a missing template and keeps an existing one — correct, because an org_admin's wording must survive every deploy. What that hides is the other direction. The sponsor letter was rewritten into a donor pitch, the deploy went green, the seed printed `kept`, and the OLD wording was still what would send; the owner opened the page and correctly reported nothing had changed. **Every signal said success and the change had not landed** — which is worse than an error, because there is nothing to investigate. The blunt fix (`--reset`) was unavailable for the reason the rule exists: six production rows carried real human edits and would have been flattened. Two habits: (1) when a sync is deliberately one-directional, write down how the OTHER direction is performed, at the line somebody reads before running it — an escape hatch nobody can find is the same as none; (2) when a deploy is supposed to change what people receive, verify the CONTENT in production, not the fact that the job ran — `kept` and `reset` both look like success in a log. Related to the same day's seed-ordering lesson, and the same root shape: the seed is the only path from code to a stored row, so anything it declines to do is a thing that silently never happens. (Four invitation emails, 2026-08-04)
- **A watcher that never fires looks exactly like work that never finished.** The build watch filtered on an 8-character commit SHA while Cloud Build's `SHORT_SHA` is 7, so it matched nothing and span until it was killed — long after both builds had gone SUCCESS. The visible effect was that I reported "builds are running" while they had been done for some time, and the owner had to ask. **A poll loop whose condition can never be true is indistinguishable from a slow job**, so give the predicate its own sanity check before arming it — run the query once, confirm it returns rows TODAY, and only then wrap it in `until`. (Four invitation emails, 2026-08-04)

- **A voice guard scoped by TABLE fails silently the moment a row's AUDIENCE changes — scope it by what the rule PROTECTS.** Two banned-phrase lists existed, split by family: the sponsor list refused tax-relief claims, the partner list refused conduit phrasing. The split read sensibly and was even documented as a principle ("what counts as the wrong voice for a partner organisation is not what counts as the wrong voice for a donor"), which is what made it durable. It quietly stopped being true when the organisation's SPONSOR INVITATION was implemented as a `PartnerEmailTemplate`: a donor pitch, validated by the list that had never heard of tax. **The single most likely surface on the platform for "your gift is tax deductible" was the only one not checking for it** — and HalaTuju holds no LHDN s44(6) approval, so that sentence is a false claim about the reader's own tax position. Nothing failed, no test went red, and the gap was invisible from either module: `partner_comms` looked complete, `sponsor_comms` looked complete. It surfaced only from asking, while rewriting the letter, *which guard actually runs on save for this row?* Two habits: (1) when a rule is filed per-family/per-module/per-table, ask what the filing assumes about that container's contents, and re-ask it every time content moves in; (2) rules that are TRUE-OR-FALSE about the world (a tax claim, a legal status, a safety statement) belong in one shared home, while rules that are genuinely about tone or audience can stay local — the first kind does not vary by reader and should never be reachable by only some of them. (Four invitation emails, 2026-08-04)
- **Splitting one template into several is only "changes nothing" if you seed the copies IDENTICALLY and say so.** `invite_staff` addressed admins and reviewers with one letter whose `{role_label}` already read correctly for both. Splitting it into `invite_admin` and `invite_reviewer` is a shape change, not a copy change, so both were seeded with the same words and a subtest asserts byte-identity per role. The temptation is to improve the wording in the same move — which would make the diff unreviewable, because a reader could no longer tell which differences came from the split and which from an edit. **Ship the split, then let the owner edit; the split's whole value is that they now CAN.** (Four invitation emails, 2026-08-04)

Cross-cutting lessons from sprint retrospectives. Only items that affect future work regardless of feature area.

- **A cheap short-circuit in front of a real guard makes the guard untestable by the obvious test — bite it, or you are only testing the short-circuit.** The once-only acceptance hook is a conditional UPDATE filtered on the column still being NULL; in front of it sits `if admin.first_seen_at is None`, which satisfies every sequential test. Removing the conditional entirely left the suite green, because nothing exercised the RACE the conditional exists for: two requests that both read the row while it was NULL, which is what a console does when a session establishes in two tabs. **The general form: when a guard has a fast path in front of it, the fast path will absorb your test and the guard will go unexercised.** Write the test against the mechanism (two independent instances, both believing they are first), not the behaviour you have in mind — and the only reliable way to tell the difference is to break the guard and watch. (Invitations sprint 1, 2026-08-03)

- **When one list feeds two tables, say whether they PARTITION or OVERLAP before writing either.** The Invitations page drew its worklist and its roster from the same unfiltered rows, so a person with an unanswered invitation appeared twice and inflated the category count with people who had never signed in. The approved mock showed them in one place; I read it as two views of everything rather than two disjoint sets, which is the sort of thing a mock states by omission and nobody notices. **Ask of any two-table screen: can the same row appear in both? If not, one of them needs an explicit exclusion, and a rendered test asserting exactly one occurrence** — a source-shape guard cannot see a duplicate. (Invitations sprint 2, 2026-08-03)

- **A test that asserts a call REFUSES has not tested the refusal — it has tested the raise. Read what it SAYS.** Pause was exhaustively unit-tested and bite-checked, and every one of those tests asserted only that assignment raised. None read the message. Walking it end-to-end on production found that a paused reviewer was refused with `not_reviewer`, which the console renders as "You can only assign to a reviewer" about somebody who is one and has simply stepped back. The cause is a shape worth recognising: `_can_review` folds THREE independent facts (inactive, paused, wrong role) into a single boolean, and the caller had no choice but to report the first of them. **When a predicate collapses several reasons into one False, any caller that turns it into a user-facing message will state the wrong reason** — so either return the reason, or reconstruct it at the point where a human will read it. And the error was reachable only from a page loaded BEFORE somebody was paused, which is exactly the case no test writes and every real console produces. (Request #10, 2026-08-03)

- **Walk a state-changing feature through the real system before calling it delivered, even when the unit tests are exhaustive — especially then.** Pause had bite-checked guards on both directions and 27 passing tests; the live walk still found a defect within minutes, because it exercised the one thing tests do not: what a person is told. Doing it safely is a method, not a risk to accept blindly — record the exact baseline first, choose a target whose revert restores the real world (an UNASSIGNED application, so an un-assign puts it back), attempt the dangerous step last, and finish by re-reading the subject and comparing it to the baseline programmatically rather than by eye. (Request #10, 2026-08-03)

- **When a check compares a record against the document the record was COPIED FROM, agreement means "we read this once", not "this is verified" — and the tick it produces is strongest exactly where the evidence is weakest.** #127 had no catalogue course and an institution written straight off her offer letter, so the institution check compared the letter with itself, agreed, and gave her the only Institution tick among the PISMP students — the one record nobody had actually checked. Meanwhile the students whose records were RIGHT lost their ticks, because their letters name the umbrella programme while their records name the specific option, which shares no distinctive token and so read as a disagreement. **The system was rewarding having nothing on file and punishing precision.** Two habits: (1) for any "does A match B?" check, ask where A came from — if the answer is "from B", the comparison is circular and must return *unknown*, not *match*; (2) a verification tick needs an INDEPENDENT source, so when a field is auto-filled from a document, every check that reads it inherits that document's authority and stops being a second opinion. The codebase already knew this on a neighbouring axis — `_declared_pathway` deliberately ignores an `offer_letter_auto` programme "to break the circularity" — and the same reasoning simply had not been carried to the institution. (Request #9, 2026-08-01)
- **A guard written for one shape will be applied by the code to every shape that happens to share a field — measure before you believe your own scoping.** The circularity guard above was meant for tertiary records with no catalogue course. It keyed on `chosen_programme.source` being offer-derived, which is ALSO true of every matric and STPM record (request #7 had just made `confirm_pathway` write them that way, hours earlier). So it changed **72 records instead of 4**: it stripped the institution status from every pre-U row and, where the institution was the only agreeing field, turned green Pathway chips grey. Nothing failed; the tests passed; the shape I had in my head was simply narrower than the predicate I wrote. It was caught only because the analysis had promised a before/after on live data and I ran it before saying a word — the audit re-derives both the old and new answer in ONE pass, which is stronger than two runs a deploy apart because it removes drift as an explanation. **Rule: when a change alters a DERIVED value, the blast radius is not the records you were thinking about — it is every record whose fields satisfy your predicate, and the only honest way to know the difference is to compute both answers over real data.** (Request #9, 2026-08-01)

- **A test that derives its fixture the same way the code under test does cannot detect a defect in that derivation — it can only fail, confusingly, inside the window.** Three billing test files computed the month under test with `timezone.now().strftime('%Y-%m')`, exactly as the endpoint did, while the DATA was grouped by Postgres in Malaysian time. So for the eight hours after Malaysian midnight on the 1st, seven tests went red — and they read as flakiness rather than as a finding, because the two attractive explanations ("pre-existing", "month-boundary artefact") are both true and both stop you looking. The tests were not wrong about the world; they had **inherited the code's misunderstanding**, which is worse than having no test, because a red suite nobody trusts is one people learn to skim. Rule: **when a test and the code compute the same quantity, at least one of them must get it from somewhere else** — a fixed instant, a stored value, the database — or the test only asserts that the code agrees with itself. The fix here pins all three computations at the exact instant they used to diverge (2026-08-31 18:00 UTC = 2026-09-01 02:00 MYT), which is the shape that would have caught it on any day of the month. (TD-209, 2026-08-01)

- **"A stored field with no surface is a defect" extends to a SWITCH with no surface — and the tell is that you picked your pattern from the wrong neighbour.** The student's partner-assignment email shipped as a hard-coded sender behind a settings flag, which is exactly how the ~30 other student emails in `emails.py` work, so it felt like house style. The owner opened Sources → Partner emails, saw five rows, and asked where it was: it was on, sending, and invisible — no switch, no wording, nothing on any screen admitting it existed. The platform HAS a surface for this family (a stored template with a switch and an editor an owner operates); I copied the mechanism from the emails that have never needed switching instead of from the ones that are operated. Two habits: (1) when a feature can be on or off, name in the plan **which screen shows its state**, and treat "none" as an unfinished design rather than a simpler one; (2) when you reach for an existing pattern, ask what the SOURCE of that pattern has in common with your case — "both are student emails" was true and irrelevant; "both are things an owner switches" was the property that mattered. The fix that holds is making the surface authoritative: the wording the owner edits IS what sends, so the screen cannot drift from the behaviour. (Request #3, 2026-08-01)
- **Moving content onto an existing mechanism inherits everything that mechanism assumes about the content — and a language assumption is invisible in the output.** The student email is bilingual, EN above MS. Re-homing it on the partner-template machinery flattened it into one editable body, which was right, and rendered the Malay half as "Program BrightPath Bursary" signed "The BrightPath Bursary Team" — because `programme_name` and `team_signoff` are single tokens that resolve to English, every previous template in that family being English throughout. Nothing failed; the copy still looked bilingual and only the brand words were wrong, in the half that the reader who needs it most would read. Rule: **before moving content onto a shared mechanism, write down what that mechanism assumes** (one language, one recipient, one tenant, plain text) and check each assumption against the content you are moving. Here the fix was ms variants of the two brand tokens, so the Malay half stays inside per-organisation branding rather than hard-coding a brand literal into stored copy. (Request #3, 2026-08-01)

- **A default nobody passes is invisible to every test you write from the code — render the artefact and read it as its recipient.** The new partner-assignment email went out from `interview@halatuju.xyz`, stamped with interview unsubscribe headers, on a message with nothing to do with interviews. `_send_html` defaults `from_email` to the interview alias because interview mail is its main caller, so the correct call and the wrong call look identical at the call site and **the wrong one is shorter**. Eleven tests asserted things inside the body and none could see it; worse, the golden snapshot would have recorded the wrong sender as the expected value, because a snapshot pins whatever it is given rather than whatever is right. It surfaced only from printing the rendered email and reading it top to bottom — subject, sender, body — the way the student would. Two habits: (1) for anything that leaves the system (an email, an export, a webhook payload), read one rendered instance end to end before shipping, including the metadata nobody names in the code; (2) when a shared helper has an opinionated default, the audit question is "which callers DIDN'T pass this?", not "which callers passed the wrong thing?" (Request #3, 2026-08-01)
- **A paid feature that ships switched off is a non-delivery dressed as caution.** Every comparable email in this codebase dark-launches OFF, and copying that pattern for the student's partner-assignment notice would have felt like good practice. But each of those is dark because something outside the code is genuinely unresolved — reviewers had not given non-objection to sharing their contact details; an org_admin was still writing the wording. Here nothing was pending: the request had been quoted, accepted and paid for, and its whole content is "the student must be told". The general form: **a flag's default should be argued from what is unresolved, not from what the neighbouring flags happen to be set to** — and when nothing is unresolved, ON is the honest default with the switch kept as a way to stop it, not as a way to avoid starting. The corollary caught a second bug in the same breath: it must not share the neighbouring feature's flag either, because that flag answers a different question ("what do ORGANISATIONS receive?") and would silently withhold something a student is owed. (Request #3, 2026-08-01)

- **A diagnosis you wrote down last session is a HYPOTHESIS, and the record it describes can refute it in one query.** The sprint brief said #119's pre-U block had been SKIPPED, and named the guard. Her stored programme reads "Tingkatan Enam" at "Kolej Tingkatan Enam Sri Istana" while her letter shouts "KOLEJ TINGKATAN ENAM SRI ISTANA" — that pairing is produced by nothing except the block the brief said never ran. So the block ran, filled her stream and college correctly on 17 July, and something WIPED them afterwards: a completely different defect, in a different file, that the planned fix would not have touched. What made the wrong brief credible is that it was mine, written carefully, with real evidence behind the other half of it (#32 genuinely was a skip). The cheap habit that caught it: **before changing a line, ask what the CURRENT stored value proves about which code paths have already run** — normalised text, a cleaned name, a canonical spelling are all fingerprints of a specific branch, and they outrank any recollection. (Requests #7/#8, 2026-08-01)
- **When a fix guards ONE field, count the fields the same writer touches — the guard's own comment will name the bug so precisely that nobody asks how far it extends.** The #117 clobber was diagnosed exactly right in July ("the profile carried an empty chosen_programme, an unrelated edit fired the two-way sync, and copy_pathway blindly overwrote the app's offer-confirmed programme with `{}`") and fixed for `chosen_programme` alone. `confirm_pathway` writes three more fields through the same door, so the identical wipe stayed live beside a comment describing it. This is the "rule enforced at a caller" lesson (2026-07-30) with the roles swapped: there the invariant was asserted in one of several callers; here the guard sits in the one right place and covers one of several fields. Same tell, worth generalising: **whenever a fix is scoped to a single field/caller/branch, write down what the OTHER members of that set are and why each is safe** — if you cannot, the fix is incomplete rather than minimal. (Requests #7/#8, 2026-08-01)
- **A guard against destructive input must refuse the input, not freeze the destination — and the difference decides whether a real user gets stranded.** The obvious fix for "a blank profile erases the offer's reading" is to freeze the pathway fields once the programme is offer-confirmed. It works, it is one line, and it silently drops a student's own correction to her stream or school for ever, with no error and no screen saying so. Refusing only the BLANK is strictly narrower: a blank carries no information, so declining to copy one cannot lose an intention, while every non-empty edit still lands. Same shape as request #6's disabled Save (a control wrongly disabled strands work with no way to keep it) — **the dangerous direction is usually the opposite of the reported bug**, and the test that matters asserts the permissive case still works, not the restrictive one. (Requests #7/#8, 2026-08-01)
- **A suite that goes red on the 1st of the month is telling you about production, not about the tests.** Seven billing/usage tests failed today for a reason unrelated to the sprint: `available_months()` groups usage by `TIME_ZONE='Asia/Kuala_Lumpur'` while the endpoint defaults "this month" from `timezone.now().strftime('%Y-%m')` — a UTC instant formatted without conversion. For the eight hours after Malaysian midnight on the 1st, the two disagree, and the billing page defaults to a month the data has already left. The tempting readings were "flaky" and "pre-existing, not mine", and both are true and both stop you looking. What it actually is: a live clock-consistency defect on a money surface that was switched on yesterday, found for free by a suite that happened to run at the wrong hour. **Rule: when a failure correlates with the clock or the calendar, characterise the PRODUCTION consequence before filing it as noise** — a date-dependent test is usually a date-dependent behaviour. (Requests #7/#8, 2026-08-01)

- **A `git push` is a REQUEST to deploy. Never write "deployed", "shipped" or "live" until the build has finished and you have READ its status.** I announced request #6 as built and deployed, wrote its completion report, and started planning the next request — while the web build had failed on a TypeScript error (`ContractClauseData[]` is not assignable to `Record<string, unknown>[]`: an INTERFACE has no index signature). The owner found it by looking at the screen and asking why nothing had changed. Two things make this worse than an ordinary slip: (1) **the claim is what stops anyone else looking** — nobody re-checks a thing that has been called done, so it would have sat broken until it caused a more confusing second problem; (2) it devalues every other status line, because "deployed" that sometimes means "pushed" cannot be read at face value. The mechanical gap is worth naming too: **`next lint` and `jest` are not the deploy gate and neither type-checks** — `npx next build` is, and this repo's noisy local `tsc` makes it easy to believe otherwise. Rule: run `next build` before pushing a frontend change, then poll `gcloud builds list` until the build leaves WORKING and report what it actually says — including for the FIX, which is a claim as much as the original. (Request #6, 2026-08-01)
- **A failure mode you have already WRITTEN DOWN is not a risk you may quietly accept — it is a route known to be closed.** I documented that `--bootstrap-login` must open its OWN Supabase session because two clients cannot share one rotation family, and that reusing the browser's refresh token would sign the owner out. An hour later the password grant turned out to be impossible for that account, and my fallback was… to copy the browser's refresh token. I even warned the owner it might sign them out. It failed on first use (`Invalid Refresh Token: Already Used`) and cost a round of console instructions given to a non-technical person. The tell is precise and easy to catch: **if the sentence in your head is "I know this is fragile, but it's the only thing left", the honest reading is "I have not yet found the route", not "fragile will do".** The route that worked (minting a session with the service-role key) needed no browser at all and existed the whole time. (TD-206 follow-up, 2026-08-01)
- **When a comment justifies a choice by claiming the alternative is broken, BREAK THE ALTERNATIVE FIRST.** I wrote that a filtered `Count` was necessary because `.exclude(rel__a=…, rel__b=…)` would miscount across a multi-valued relation, then bit that claim and watched every test pass: a SINGLE multi-condition `exclude()` compiles to one `NOT EXISTS` on the same joined row, which is exactly the question. The trap belongs to CHAINED `.exclude(a).exclude(b)`. I had promoted a real scar from an adjacent bug into a general rule and written it into the source as fact. Code comments are the one artefact no test ever checks — a wrong one survives every run, forever, and the next reader inherits it as knowledge rather than as an opinion. Rule: a comment of the form "X, not Y, because Y would break" is a testable assertion; make Y break, or write "X; Y is probably equivalent, untested". (TD-205, 2026-08-01)
- **"Stored but never displayed" has an outer ring: FETCHED but never displayed — and it hides better, because the network tab shows the call being made.** `useNavProbes` called the requests-count endpoint on every shell mount and used the response only to decide whether the feature was live, discarding `count` in the same `.then()`. The endpoint was correct, its tests passed, the request was visibly firing — and four bug reports sat unnoticed for two days because the number reached no pixel. A stale comment two files away ("the Administration hub still probes for ITSELF, it needs the request COUNT") made the omission look deliberate; the hub had stopped calling it entirely. Two habits: (1) for any endpoint whose whole purpose is a NUMBER, grep the field name — not the function name — through the client and confirm something renders it; (2) when a comment explains why a value is not used HERE because it is used THERE, check THERE before believing it. (TD-205, 2026-08-01)
- **Bite-check the CLAIM in your comment, not just the behaviour of your code — a justification is a testable assertion and mine was false.** I wrote that a filtered `Count` was necessary because `.exclude(rel__a=…, rel__b=…)` across a multi-valued relation would miscount, citing this project's real multi-valued-annotate scars. Swapping in the `exclude` form to watch a test fail... passed every test. Django compiles a SINGLE multi-condition `exclude()` into one `NOT EXISTS` with both conditions on the same joined row, which is exactly the question; the trap belongs to CHAINED `.exclude(a).exclude(b)`, which asks two independent rows two independent questions. So a plausible war story from an adjacent bug had been promoted to a rule and written into the code as fact. This landed the same day as the lesson above it about confident wrong explanations — the difference is that a wrong comment survives every test run, forever, and the next reader inherits it as knowledge. **If a comment says "X, not Y, because Y would break" — make Y break before you write it.** (TD-205, 2026-08-01)
- **An error message you write for a call you did not make must PRINT the response and add your guess second — never instead.** Twice in one afternoon a hand-written message named a cause the server had already contradicted. `--bootstrap-login` said *"Supabase refused the password grant (400). Check the email and password"*, so the owner checked a password — for an account that has none, because the super admin is GOOGLE-ONLY (`auth.users.encrypted_password` is null) and no password grant could ever succeed for it. Then a minted token was refused with *"tokens expire after about an hour — get a fresh one"*, so the instinct was to fetch another — but the token was valid and unexpired, and the API had plainly said `Admin access required`, because it belonged to an ANONYMOUS session. In both cases the body carried the answer and my code threw it away in favour of the failure I expected at that line. The asymmetry is the point: **a vague error makes someone investigate; a confident wrong one makes them act**, and both of mine pointed at work that could not possibly succeed. This is the same shape as the 2026-07-06 entry about confident wrong explanations, now with a mechanical rule attached: any `raise` written against a status code must interpolate the response body. Two tests pin it. (Request #4 / TD-206, 2026-08-01)
- **How an account authenticates is a row in `auth.users`, not an inference from how it was created.** I built and tested an entire password-grant login for the one account that has no password, reasoning "admins were invited by email, therefore they have passwords" from a memory note about invite emails. One query — `SELECT encrypted_password IS NOT NULL, (SELECT string_agg(provider,',') FROM auth.identities WHERE user_id = u.id) FROM auth.users u WHERE email = …` — is the same query I eventually ran to diagnose the failure, and it would have shown `google` / no password before a line was written. General rule: before building against a credential, **ask the identity store what credentials that identity actually has.** (TD-206, 2026-08-01)
- **A selector that cannot fail loudly must at least report what it selected.** `Object.keys(localStorage).find(k => k.includes('auth-token'))` looked like "find the login token"; the app keeps several matching entries, so it returned an ANONYMOUS session's token that authenticated perfectly and had no admin behind it. There was no wrong-shaped output to notice — the failure only surfaced three steps later as a 403 on a different machine. Worse, I had a non-technical owner run it, twice. The fix scans every candidate, takes only the session carrying a user email, and **prints that email back for confirmation**. The tell: any `find`/`first`/`[0]` over a collection whose members are near-identical, where picking the wrong one produces valid-looking output. (TD-206, 2026-08-01)
- **A rule keyed on what someone DECLARED will eventually contradict a rule keyed on what they SUPPLIED — and the two can disagree for weeks in silence.** `income_established` (the submission gate) is route-agnostic and accepted #106's salary cluster, so she submitted without trouble; `_verdict_income` checked her DECLARED route, found no STR, and painted the same household red. One household, two verdicts, no alarm anywhere — the officer sees green document chips beside a red summary and has no way to tell which is lying. The fix that holds is not "add the missing branch" but **making the second surface call the first surface's predicate**, because one derivation cannot disagree with itself. The tell is a pair of functions answering the same question where one of them reads a *declaration field* (`income_route`, `kind`, `type`) to decide which evidence counts. (Request #4, 2026-08-01)
- **When a spec lists three trigger conditions and the code implements two, that is not a missing feature — it is a live defect with a specification already written for it.** `str-proof-spec.md` §6 rule 2 has said "rejected / wrong_type / **absent**" since it was written; only the first two were built, and the third was the one a real student was stuck behind. It read as new work for months because nobody diffed the enumeration in the prose against the branches in the code. Cheap habit when touching any spec-backed engine: **count the cases in the spec sentence and count the branches, and if they differ, that is the sprint.** (Request #4, 2026-08-01)

- **Putting a second opinion on a screen makes every existing DEFAULT on that screen a claim — and defaults were written before the second opinion existed.** The Requests cockpit gained an engineer's analysis directly above a triage form whose classification was hard-coded to `feature`/`sprint`. The AI had read request #4 as a bug, the analysis agreed, and the form still offered Feature — three opinions on one screen, one of them a constant nobody had revisited. It is not cosmetic: a bug is FREE and a feature is PRICED, so accepting the default silently converts one into the other. I had just built a prefill for the quote hours from that same analysis and never asked what ELSE on the surface had a default that now disagreed with the data beside it. **The habit: when you add a computed opinion to a screen, enumerate every control already on it that has a hard-coded starting value, and ask whether that value now contradicts something visible.** A default is an opinion with no author and no date, which is exactly why it survives review. Corollary: seed from the data behind a touched-ref so the human's choice always wins, and test the no-data case explicitly — the fallback is where the old default legitimately lives on. (TD-204, 2026-07-31)

- **Place a new panel by walking the surface in order, not by naming the one block it must precede.** I put the analysis panel "above the quote form", which was the rule I had in mind, and it landed below the TRIAGE form — so the owner was asked to classify a request before reading the thing that tells them the classification. The reasoning was locally correct and globally wrong, because I never listed what else sat between the top of the panel and the block I had named. Same shape as any "insert X before Y" instruction: **Y is a landmark, not a position.** Read the ordered list of siblings once before choosing an index, and state the placement as a position in that list ("after the AI draft, before triage") rather than as a relation to a single neighbour. (TD-204, 2026-07-31)

- **Any hostname, connection string or identifier you assemble from a remembered PATTERN is a guess — ask the service that knows before you spend attempts on it.** Running a command against production failed because Supabase's direct database host is IPv6-only from this machine. I reached for the session pooler and assembled `aws-0-<region>.pooler.supabase.com` from memory, failed, tried a variant, failed, and only then called `list_projects` — which returns the region and would have told me in one call. Two wasted attempts on a production path, and each failure looked like a credentials problem rather than a naming one, which is what makes this expensive: **a wrong assembled identifier fails with an error about the thing you assembled it FOR** (here "tenant/user not found"), so it sends you looking at permissions. The rule is narrow and mechanical: if any part of a connection target came out of your head rather than out of a lookup, do the lookup first. (TD-204, 2026-07-31)

- **`git checkout <path>` is a destructive command, and "commit before bite-checking" is really a rule about the REVERT, not about the ritual.** Twice in one sprint I broke a guard to watch it fail, then ran `git checkout` to restore it and lost uncommitted work in the same file — the first time four hand-applied edits, the second the fix I had just written. Both times I had followed "commit first" correctly for every bite that came from the plan, and skipped it for the bite that arose mid-flow from live feedback, because the rule was filed in my head under *bite-checking* rather than under *reverting a file*. The generalisation that actually holds: **before `git checkout`, `git restore` or `git stash drop` on a path, ask what in that path is uncommitted — the command cannot distinguish your experiment from your work.** Cheap habit: `git status --short <path>` first, or `git stash` instead of `checkout` so the revert is undoable. (TD-201, 2026-07-31)

- **When two affordances write the same state through different windows, the bug lives where only ONE of them is available.** The Requests thread got a reply box (closes at acceptance) and a comment box (runs to terminal), and only the reply box cleared `awaiting_reply`. Each window was reasoned about correctly on its own; nobody asked what happens past acceptance, where the comment box is the only box — and there the flag became unclearable by construction, so a question read "Unanswered" for ever directly above its own answer and the owner's badge stayed lit on a request nobody was waiting on. Every test passed, because each tested a window in which both boxes existed. **The rule: for any state written by more than one path, enumerate the conditions where each path is unavailable, and test the state transition in each of those — the intersection where one path drops out is where permanent states hide.** A related tell: if you find yourself explaining that two controls "do the same thing but in different windows", the windows are a detail of WHEN a user may act and must not decide WHETHER the act counts. (TD-201, 2026-07-31)

- **A field allowlist protects a COLUMN and does nothing about a ROW — if the wrong audience must not see a whole record, the filter belongs at the query.** The org-facing serializer for org requests is a strict allowlist with zero model passthrough, and it was tempting to treat that as the protection for internal comments too. It is not: the serializer names `body`, and it renders an internal comment as happily as a shared one. The distinction is worth holding as a habit because the two failures look identical in review — an allowlist *appears* to be the security boundary while silently only covering half the question. **Ask of every new privacy rule: is this a field the audience may not read, or a row?** If it is a row, the filter goes in the service beside the query, once, and is asserted at both the serializer and the endpoint so bypassing it fails twice. (TD-201, 2026-07-31)

- **Making a MATCHER tolerant of a bad input leaves the bad input in the database for ever — tolerance belongs on read, normalisation belongs on write, and doing only the first hides the second.** Application #20's stored name was `SHARVANI A/ P KANAGEVELLU`; her MyKad and every document we hold read `A/P`. The space came from her own typed declaration signature, which `submit_application` promotes to `profile.name` **verbatim**. What made it permanent was our own earlier fix: her typed name had produced a false Name mismatch against her own IC, so `vision._NAME_NOISE` was taught to ignore whitespace inside a slash marker — and the comment recording that tolerance names #20 explicitly. The tolerance was RIGHT (it is the same name; a red chip would have been wrong), and it silenced the only signal that would ever have surfaced the typo. So the record sailed green through identity checks into emails, the payments CSV and a partner export, where a human finally read it. **The tell is precise and worth memorising: whenever you widen a comparison to accept a variant, ask what writes that variant and whether it should be normalised there instead — a tolerance is a decision to stop being told.** Two corollaries: (1) a field promoted from user input "verbatim" is a write boundary with no normalisation, so it deserves one the moment any reader is human-facing; (2) fix the class at the write boundary that already exists (`StudentProfile.save()` was already normalising case for exactly these readers) rather than inventing a second place to be consistent. (Parentage marker, 2026-07-30)

- **"Canonical" is a claim about a COLUMN, not about what a screen renders — before fixing data to fix a display, find the accessor that display actually calls.** I corrected `profile.name` to the MyKad spelling and told the owner it would fix the cockpit and the partner exports, quoting the model's own comment that `profile.name` "is the canonical name everything displays". The exports, yes. The cockpit, no: `serializers_admin._full_name` **prefers `declaration_name`** — the typed signature — because `profile.name` is often a Google handle, so the header kept showing the stray space and the owner screenshotted it back to me. Both fields are legitimately "the name", with opposite precedence in different layers, and the docstring asserting canonicality lives beside the column rather than beside the reader. Two habits: (1) when a data fix is meant to change a screen, grep the serializer field the screen binds to and follow it to its accessor — one `grep` for the JSON key would have shown `_full_name` in seconds; (2) treat "canonical" in a comment as a claim about that module only, because the phrase is written by whoever owns the write path and is routinely false of a read path that prefers something else. The general shape is a **fallback chain read in the opposite order by two layers**, and the tell is a field whose docstring says "prefer X over Y" anywhere in the codebase. (Parentage marker, 2026-07-30)

- **A unit test on a helper does not prove the helper is CALLED — the wiring needs its own test, and it is the one that would actually have failed.** I added `tidy_parentage_marker` with 13 unit tests covering all four markers, every spacing variant, idempotency and the no-op cases. Every one passed with the function wired into `StudentProfile.save()` — and every one would also have passed with it not wired at all. The test that carries the value is the integration one: save a profile with `A/ P`, read it back, assert `A/P`. Confirmed by unhooking the call and watching exactly that test fail while the other thirteen stayed green. Same shape as this project's standing "a stored field with no surface is a defect" finding, one layer down: **a helper with no caller is a defect, and a suite of unit tests is structurally blind to it.** Rule: for any pure function added to fix a real bug, one test must exercise it through the production entry point that the bug travelled. (Parentage marker, 2026-07-30)

- **A rule enforced at a CALLER is a rule with as many holes as there are other callers — and the docstring asserting it will be locally true, which is why nobody rereads it.** `award_amount` was cleared by the verdict recorder under the comment *"On DECLINE, clear it"*. Accurate, in that file. But a case can be declined three ways — the verdict recorder, `admin_reject` (interview/contractual) and `org_admin_reject` — and the other two left the money on the record. Two production rows carried a stale amount for eleven days. What made it survive review is that the comment reads like a statement about the SYSTEM while sitting in one branch of one handler, so anyone auditing "do we clear the amount on decline?" greps, finds the line, and stops. Two habits: (1) when you find an invariant asserted at a caller, **count the callers before believing it** — `grep` the state transition (here `status = 'rejected'`), not the field; (2) enforce it at the choke-point they share, and say in the comment that it is the choke-point and why, so the next person doesn't re-scatter it. The tell is a docstring in the imperative mood ("clear it", "stamp it", "notify them") describing a rule the surrounding function does not own. (Award-amount clear, 2026-07-30)

- **CLEARING a field is only safe if it is RECOVERABLE — check for the undo path before you null anything on a money record.** Fixing the above by simply nulling `award_amount` at the reject choke-point passes every test and introduces a worse bug. `DECLINE_COOLOFF_DAYS` is 7 on production, so `cancel_pending_decline` can reverse any `admin_reject` for a week, and for a *contractual* decline it restores a funded student **and reinstates their lapsed sponsorship** — who would then come back with no award amount, and `payments.amount_due` caps at `award − paid`, so their pay silently becomes zero. A live student quietly unpayable is strictly worse than a rejected student holding a number nobody spends. The codebase had already answered this once and I nearly missed it: `pre_decline_status` exists for exactly this reason, and its docstring says *"snapshots the pre-decline status so cancel_pending_decline can restore it exactly"* — the shape of the fix was sitting one line above the bug. General form: **before adding a destructive write, find the reversal path and ask what your write costs it**; and when the surrounding code already snapshots one field for a reversal, the honest fix is to join that snapshot, not to invent a new mechanism beside it. (Award-amount clear, 2026-07-30)

- **Never run two Django processes concurrently against the same project — the contention failures are indistinguishable from real code defects, and the standing instruction to parallelise independent tool calls will lead you straight into it.** I fired `makemigrations --check` and the courses/reports suite in one message, as genuinely independent commands. The suite came back **1252 passed, 8 errors** in `test_stpm_search.py`. The file passes 20/20 alone, so the obvious read was "pre-existing ordering artefact, not mine" — and stating that would have been wrong twice over: not pre-existing (the clean tree gives 1260/0) and not an artefact (it was my own parallelism). Only stashing and re-running the identical command on a clean tree separated the three explanations, and it took two extra runs to get an honest sentence. Two rules: (1) test and management commands are **not** independent tool calls, because they share a database and a migration state — serialise anything that touches Django; (2) when a suite fails in aggregate but passes in isolation, **reproduce on a clean tree before characterising it**, because "pre-existing" and "flaky" are the two most attractive wrong answers available and both let you stop looking. (Award-amount clear, 2026-07-30)

- **A proxy predicate for "bad data" will misclassify in BOTH directions — verify the consequence, not the shape.** Sweeping for stale award amounts I used "holds an amount AND has no accepted verdict". It caught app 15, who holds RM2,000 legitimately (a live sponsorship, 7 payment items, a disbursement — her `officer_verdict.overall` is simply blank from a draft-save the codebase explicitly permits), and it MISSED app 99, who holds RM1,000 mid-reopen and would have been the next stale row had that reopen ended in a decline. Both errors came from asking about verdict shape when the question was "is money actually attached to this record?" — so the pre-flight became `NOT EXISTS` against sponsorships, payment items and disbursements, which is the fact that decides whether clearing is safe, and the `UPDATE` re-asserted each expected amount in its `WHERE` so a value moving between read and write touches nothing. General form: **when a sweep drives a destructive write, filter on the consequence you are protecting, not the symptom that led you there** — and expect the symptom filter to have false positives that look exactly like the true ones. (Award-amount clear, 2026-07-30)

- **The EXISTENCE of a manual management command tells you nothing about whether a scheduler already runs it — check Cloud Scheduler before calling any manual step "owed".** Diagnosing three wrong eWallet IDs I told the owner the Vircle relay sheet was stale, that `sync_vircle_sheet` had to be run by hand, and hung a *time-sensitive* consequence off it (the 48h activation cron would email Vircle a stale number). The owner pushed back with one line — it is rewritten every 15 minutes. `halatuju-vircle-sheet-sync` is ENABLED on `*/15 * * * *` and had already self-healed within minutes of my correction; the whole consequence chain was downstream of a false premise. This is the FOURTH instance of the live-state family in this file (2026-06-15 flags, 2026-07-21 memory, 2026-07-22 `DECLINE_COOLOFF_DAYS`) and none of them stopped it, because those are phrased for when you already suspect you are guessing — and **reading a management command in source does not feel like guessing, it feels like reading the code.** The operational trigger, which needs no judgement: **any sentence telling a human to run something is a claim about what is NOT already automated.** `gcloud scheduler jobs list --project … --location …` first; the answer takes ten seconds and the project has 25 jobs, most of which nobody remembers. Corollary: a project with a large scheduler fleet should be assumed to automate a thing until shown otherwise, which is the opposite of the default I applied. (Vircle ID band, 2026-07-30)

- **"It's audited" is a claim about a specific log line — grep for the line; never infer it from a sibling in the same file.** I advised the owner to correct three production wallet IDs through the cockpit rather than SQL *because the endpoint was audited*. It is not: `AdminApplicationFlagsView`'s `vircle_id` branch writes no `AUDIT` line at all, while `services.set_reporting_date` — the obvious analogue, same shape, same kind of officer correction — does. So the advice bought nothing over a direct `UPDATE`, and the recommendation had to be withdrawn a turn later. **Proximity and similarity are not shared behaviour**, and a field's importance does not imply someone already instrumented it: this one decides where money goes. Two habits: (1) before asserting any endpoint is audited/logged/rate-limited/fenced, `grep` for the mechanism inside that handler, not in the module; (2) when the answer is "it isn't", that is a finding worth reporting — the gap I stumbled into here was worth more than the advice I got wrong. (Vircle ID band, 2026-07-30)

- **When a wrong value reaches the database by TRUNCATION, test the rule against what the human TYPES — not against the stored result.** The owner proposed constraining a digit of the eWallet ID; I tested whether that rule could separate the three wrong *stored 13-digit values* from the right ones, found it could not, and reported the fix inert. The owner replied "it would prevent this though", and was right: a Vircle DuitNow number is **18 digits whose first 13 ARE the eWallet ID**, the form asks for the last four, and the rule rejects what a student typing from the wrong field actually produces. I had modelled DuitNow as a rival 13-digit identifier **with no evidence** — one screenshot dissolved it, along with a "the two families will eventually collide" argument I had built on top. In the same conversation I then proposed accepting the 18-digit number and DERIVING the id from its first 13 — a structure I had observed exactly **once**, on a path that moves money. Three habits: (1) get the source artefact (the actual screen) before modelling how a human produces a bad value; (2) for any entry-error class, the unit of analysis is the KEYSTROKES, not the row; (3) never derive a payment destination from a structure seen once — reject and NAME it instead, which is both safer and better copy. (Vircle ID band, 2026-07-30)

- **Adopting a LIVE feature into a dark launch will silently switch it off unless you keep the old path.** S3 put nine sponsor emails behind two gates (a platform flag AND a per-template switch), both off by default. Three of the nine were already sending on production. Routing them through the new machinery meant every one of them evaluated to "don't send" on the first deploy — no error, no failed test, a panel of tidy switches all correctly reading "off", and sponsors simply never hearing from us again. Nobody would have found it from the inside; it surfaces as a sponsor eventually asking why they stopped getting students. The fix is one branch per adopted path, plus a test per path asserting the LEGACY sender still fires while dark. **But which condition the branch tests is the harder half, and I got it wrong first.** I keyed it on "platform gate AND this template's switch", which is fine while "off" means *not yet adopted* — and becomes a lie the moment those templates ship switched ON (as the owner then decided they should, since the emails are already active). With them on, "off" means *stop sending this*, and a fallback keyed on the switch quietly overrides it: the owner ticks the box off, the old sender carries on, the screen disagrees with the system. Keying it on the PLATFORM gate alone gives two clean worlds — before the flip nothing changes, after it the templates govern completely. General shape: **a dark launch is safe for NEW behaviour and dangerous for ADOPTED behaviour**, so list what is live today FIRST and treat each as a migration; and then ask what the flag's OFF position will MEAN to the person who owns it, because a fallback that overrides "off" is worse than no fallback — it turns a control into a lie. The tell for the first half is a sprint description containing "route the existing X through the new Y"; the tell for the second is a seeded-on template. (Sponsor S3, 2026-07-28)

- **A namespace passed as a prop looks exactly like a missing i18n key to a static scanner.** Generalising the template editor for a second family meant passing `prefix="admin.sources.emails"` and building `${prefix}.subject` inside the component. The i18n hygiene guard scans source for `admin.sources.*` string literals and asserts each resolves to a STRING; the namespace resolves to an object, so the build failed on a prop doing exactly what it should. The guard was right to fire — it cannot tell a namespace from a leaf — so the fix is to teach it the idiom explicitly (an allowlist of namespace props) rather than loosen the regex, which would stop it catching the real thing it exists for. Worth knowing before the next component is parameterised by i18n prefix: **the scanner's contract is "every literal that looks like a key is a leaf", and prefix-props break that contract by design.** (Sponsor S3, 2026-07-28)

- **A flag derived from a DOWNSTREAM artefact reads false in exactly the state it exists to describe.** `finance_check_required` tells the sponsor screen whether the credit chain has a middle step. I computed it from the sponsor's WALLETS — and a wallet only exists once a credit is `confirmed`, so for a credit that is recorded and awaiting its signatures (the only state where the answer changes what the screen draws) there was no wallet to read and the flag said "no finance step". The test I wrote passed because the fixture confirmed a credit first. General shape: when a flag describes work IN PROGRESS, never derive it from a record that only appears when the work is FINISHED — derive it from the thing that exists throughout (here the credit itself, and the membership that precedes it). Cheap tell during review: the flag's inputs and the states it must distinguish have different lifetimes. It was latent for a day and only harmless because the feature it gates (a `finance` admin) has never been switched on — which is the other half of the lesson: a dormant feature hides the bugs in the code that reads it, so its first activation is a deploy, not a config change. (Sponsor S2, 2026-07-28)

- **When a role gains its first write power, the role matrix and the manual chapter are both now lying — and the older lie is the one nobody will look for.** S2 made `admin` (Admin-General) the maker of a wallet credit, so I went to update its manual chapter and found it opening with *"you have a read-only view… you don't act on them"* — which had ALREADY been false since 2026-07-16, when the Payments module made that same role the maker of a payment run. The role-matrix table said "View all (read-only)" while the Payments section three paragraphs below it said "CREATE / EDIT / CANCEL: `admin` + `org_admin`". Two facts in one document, contradicting each other, for twelve days. The currency rule ("when a role power changes, its chapter + FAQ update in the same change") had been followed for the *new* Payments section and skipped for the *summary table* — because a summary table doesn't feel like documentation of the thing you just built. So: **whenever a sprint adds a power to a role, re-read that role's SUMMARY row and its chapter's opening sentence, not only the section you are adding** — a per-feature section can be added correctly while the one-line characterisation above it rots, and the characterisation is what a new person reads first. (Sponsor S2, 2026-07-28)

- **A conversion figure of exactly zero is a claim about your INSTRUMENTATION before it is a claim about the world — check the attribution path before you believe it.** The sponsor detail page listed 8 people invited and 0 joined. Five of them had joined weeks earlier and were sitting in the sponsors table under their own names. `attribute_referral` only ever fired when someone registered through `/sponsor?ref=<code>`; anyone who read the invite and then went to the site themselves — which is most people — left the row at `invited` for ever. The defect shipped in June and was invisible until a screen finally displayed the number. Two habits: (1) when a funnel reports a perfect 0 (or a perfect 100%), find the write that is supposed to move it and ask what fraction of real journeys reaches that line — an attribution that depends on a specific click is a minority path by construction; (2) when you build the SCREEN for an existing metric, spot-check two or three rows against the underlying tables before trusting the aggregate, because the first display of a number is also its first audit. General form: instrumentation that only records the instrumented route measures the route, not the outcome. (Sponsor fixes, 2026-07-28)

- **A column that starts recording today makes every historical row read as a negative fact — so the empty-state copy must say "no record", never "did not happen".** `last_seen_at` was added on 27 July and the list rendered its null as "Not since joining", which states that an approved sponsor has never once come back. For eight of nine production rows that was not a finding, it was the absence of one: the field simply did not exist while they were visiting. The mechanism was verified working (a real visit stamped correctly, a second was correctly throttled) — the code was right and the sentence was false, which is the combination no test catches, because tests assert the branch was taken, not that the words are true. Whenever you add a field that will be null for all existing rows, write the null copy from the READER's position ("No sign-in recorded", plus since when) and keep the confident phrasing for values you actually hold. Same shape as any new audit column, consent stamp or verification flag: the backfill you cannot do has to be admitted in the copy. (Sponsor fixes, 2026-07-28)

- **Two multi-valued `annotate()`s in one queryset multiply each other — and `distinct=True`, the reflex fix, is silently WRONG for a `Sum`.** Adding a students count beside the money on the sponsors list looked like one more argument to the existing `annotate()`. It is a second join: a sponsor with 2 credits and 3 students returns 6 rows, so the money reads 3× and the count 2×. Reaching for `Count(distinct=True)` fixes the count and leaves the sum inflated; reaching for `Sum(distinct=True)` is worse than the bug — it sums DISTINCT VALUES, so two genuine RM10,000 credits become RM10,000, which looks entirely plausible on screen and is unrecoverable by eye. Count the second relation in its own aggregate query keyed by id and attach it in Python: one extra query, no fan-out, no N+1. The test that protects this must have BOTH relations non-trivial *and* two equal amounts — an equal pair is the only fixture that distinguishes the fan-out from the `distinct` cure. (Sponsor fixes, 2026-07-28)

- **A `Sum()` aggregate and a `DecimalField` read do not produce the same string — a payload mixing them renders two money formats on one card.** `str(row.amount)` gives `'3000.00'` (the column's scale); `str(qs.aggregate(Sum('amount'))['s'])` gives `'20000'` for the same column, because the aggregate comes back as an unquantised `Decimal`. The sponsor wallet showed "RM 20000" beside "RM 3,000.00" in adjacent tiles, and only a test that asserted the exact string caught it — a `Number()` in the template would have hidden it while still printing `RM 20000`. Quantise at the API boundary (one `_money()` helper), not in the template, so every money string the API emits has the same shape whatever produced it. General form: when two code paths compute "the same" value, assert the SERIALISED form, not the numeric one. (Sponsor S1, 2026-07-27)

- **Before adding a payload to an admin screen, grep for the dict that already serialises that model — the second copy is where the next field goes missing.** The sponsor detail page needed a credits list, and I hand-wrote one: 13 fields, all correct. `_credit_dict` already existed 3000 lines further down in the same file, serving the credit endpoints, and was a strict superset (it also carries `is_spendable`). Two copies of a money payload means the next column gets added to one of them, and the divergence shows up as a field that is present on one screen and absent on another with no error anywhere. Python resolves module-level names at call time, so reusing a helper defined *below* your function is free — there is no ordering excuse. The same check applies to the "which wallets does this sponsor hold" query, which I extracted (`_wallet_programmes`) rather than write twice. (Sponsor S1, 2026-07-27)

- **A fault-injection test must break the thing it claims to test, not something upstream of it.** To prove the `last_seen_at` stamp could never break the sponsor portal, I patched `views_sponsor.Sponsor.objects` and asserted a 200. It failed — but for the wrong reason: `SponsorMixin.get_sponsor` uses the same manager and runs BEFORE the stamp, and is deliberately *not* wrapped. So the test was asserting that an unwrapped call swallows errors, which it must not. Had it passed (say, if `get_sponsor` had been cached) it would have been worse: a green test proving nothing about the guard. Patch at the narrowest point the code under test actually calls, or test the unit directly — a swallow-guard is a unit-level claim and does not need the view to prove it. (Sponsor S1, 2026-07-27)

- **"Add the missing rows" — verify they are missing from the table you think they are missing from.** A roadmap item read "add `course_institutions` rows for #132/#136 — a data fix": I had classified them as catalogue courses with zero campus links, because `CourseInstitution` returned nothing for their ids. In fact those ids are not in the `courses` table at all — they are **`stpm_courses`** rows (the separate post-STPM catalogue), each already carrying its university. Nothing was missing; the resolvers were querying one of two catalogues. The tell I read past twice was the trailing `#` in their stored names ("… dengan Kepujian #"), which is e-Panduan's STPM formatting. Two rows of data entry would have looked like a fix and left every future STPM-degree applicant broken, where a four-line fallback fixed the class with **no production write**. General form: an empty query result means "not in THIS table", never "missing from the system" — and a planned data insert deserves the same "is my premise true?" check as a planned code change, because a data fix leaves no test behind to catch the misdiagnosis. (Institution Sprint 3, 2026-07-26)

- **When adding a catalogue/lookup helper, grep the module for one that already answers the question — and make the existing one delegate rather than adding a sibling.** In one sprint I created `offer_pathway.sole_catalogue_institution` beside the existing `card_display.catalogue_single_institution` (same question, same semantics), then wrote `_campus_rows` and left `sole_catalogue_institution` with its own inline `CourseInstitution` query. Both divergences meant the STPM-catalogue fix would have landed in one function and not the others — the same shape as the acronym regression the day before, which is already a lesson in this file. It recurred twice more within 24 hours *for the author of that lesson*, which is the useful datum: the principle doesn't fire on its own, so it needs to be a mechanical step ("grep before you add a lookup"), not a thing to remember. (Institution Sprint 3, 2026-07-26)

- **Making a previously-EMPTY field populated is a change to every reader that has only ever seen it empty — and the risk is in the readers, not the write.** Sprint 1 filled `chosen_programme.institution` for #48, shipped green on 4559 tests, and broke that student's live record within the hour: `offer_pathway_match` compares the recorded institution to the offer letter's by distinctive-token overlap, and "Universiti Tun Hussein Onn Malaysia" shares no token with the letter's "UTHM - KAMPUS (CAWANGAN PAGOH)". While the field was blank the comparison returned `unknown` — nothing to compare, benignly silent. Filling it produced `clash`: a correct pathway read `mismatch`, the Pathway chip went red, the Institution tick vanished, a red chip docked the verdict band, and Check 2 asked the student to confirm a pathway that was already right. **The tests covered the new writer exhaustively and covered nothing that CONSUMES its output**, so the transition blank→populated was expressed nowhere. Two habits: (1) before filling a field that is currently blank in production, grep every reader and ask what each does with a value it has never received — a `''`-means-unknown branch is the tell; (2) write the test as an INVARIANT ACROSS the transition ("the pathway verdict is the same whether the institution is blank or filled"), not as an assertion about the new value — that is the only shape that fails before the fix and passes after. (Institution must-fill, 2026-07-26; the incident is 2026-07-25)

- **Two functions that answer the same question must share the predicate, or the one you are not looking at stays wrong.** In the SAME sprint I taught `offer_contradicts_course_institution` that a catalogue ACRONYM identifies an institution — precisely because "UTHM" shares no token with the full name — and left `_field_status`, which paints the officer's Pathway chip and drives the verdict, comparing the same two strings by token overlap. So the writer's guard was acronym-aware and the display comparison was acronym-blind, on the same pair of strings, hours apart. Fixed by extracting one `_refers_to_campus` used by both. General tell: if you find yourself writing a second "do these two refer to the same thing?" test in a module that already has one, you are creating a divergence, not adding a feature — and the copy you did NOT write the test for is the one that reaches the user. (Institution must-fill, 2026-07-26)

- **When you fix "a passenger fact inherits its host function's exits", check whether the host carries MORE passengers — and when you extract one, check whether the block you're replacing did TWO jobs.** The 2026-07-23 reporting-date fix hoisted the date above `autofill_pathway_from_offer`'s four pathway guards and wrote the lesson up as a general shape (entry #11 in this file). It then left the INSTITUTION sitting in exactly the same position, one fact below, in the same function — so the institution sat below the same four guards the date had escaped. (Diagnostic caveat, added 2026-07-26: those guards were NOT what left #48 blank — its name tolerance had shipped 2026-07-08 and `_name_status` recomputes live, so the guard passes. The blank came from `catalogue_institution` refusing a hint-less answer. I asserted the guard as the cause from a STORED `student_verdict` without checking that the value is derived — read the live computation, not the cached one, before naming a cause.) The corollary bit on the way out: the tail block being replaced was doing *fill a blank* AND *normalise a drifted value to the catalogue's canonical spelling*, and moving the whole block up deleted the second behaviour — caught only because a pre-existing test (`test_institution_aligned_to_catalogue_even_when_locked`) covered it. Two habits: (1) when you hoist one passenger out of a guarded function, **enumerate every other write in that function's tail** and ask which of them are also independent facts; (2) before deleting a block you have "moved", list the distinct jobs it performed — a block that both fills and normalises needs a split, not a relocation. (Institution must-fill, Sprint 1, 2026-07-25)

- **"Couldn't verify" is not "contradicts" — a guard built on the absence of a positive match will fire on the very case it was written to allow.** The institution writer needed to refuse a fill when the student's offer letter names a place that is not a campus of their declared course (#11 Politeknik Ungku Omar against a UPNM asasi). The first cut asked `if not catalogue_institution(cid, offer_inst)` — but that function answers "can I verify these are the same place?", and its `''` covers *unverifiable* (an acronym, an OCR variant, a campus suffix) as well as *contradictory*. It therefore rejected #48's own letter, "UTHM - KAMPUS (CAWANGAN PAGOH)", which shares no distinctive token with "Universiti Tun Hussein Onn Malaysia" — the exact record the sprint existed to fix, caught by a test written from the real prod value rather than an invented one. The fix was a purpose-built predicate checking identity three ways (tokens, parenthetical-stripped name, catalogue **acronym**). General rule: when you need "these disagree", write that predicate; never spell it as the negation of "these provably agree", and prefer the entity's own identifiers (acronyms, codes) over name-token overlap when the catalogue carries them. (Institution must-fill, Sprint 1, 2026-07-25)

- **Pin the encoding on every fixture read/write — `Path.read_text()` defaults to the PLATFORM encoding, so a golden file with non-Latin copy is unreadable on a Windows dev box while passing on CI.** `test_email_branding.py` wrote and read its 113-email golden with bare `write_text`/`read_text`; on cp1252 the first Tamil byte raised `UnicodeDecodeError`, leaving the suite red locally for reasons that had nothing to do with the code under test (and indistinguishable, at a glance, from a real golden mismatch). Any fixture holding ms/ta copy — and in this codebase that is most of them — must pass `encoding='utf-8'` explicitly in BOTH directions, or its bytes depend on which machine ran the suite. Same family as the Node-26 `sessionStorage` rot (TD-171): an environment-dependent test failure costs the next sprint real time deciding whether it owns the break. (Institution must-fill, Sprint 1, 2026-07-25)

- **A byte-identity snapshot only protects the functions it actually renders — a mechanical refactor that also touches functions OUTSIDE the snapshot set has an invisible blast radius there.** Sprint 5 routed brand literals through a seam using a string-concat form that (harmlessly for plain strings) closes and reopens the literal around the seam value. That form SILENTLY DROPS the `f`-prefix on the reopened segment, so any f-string with a `{placeholder}` *after* the brand — `f'A reminder … {programme} … {soon_en}'` — rendered a literal `{soon_en}`. For the 3 student-facing emails this was caught the instant the goldens ran; but the SAME bug hit `send_payment_countersign_email` and the Vircle-activation email, which are NOT in the 113-snapshot set, so the goldens were green while `{month}` and `{run.reference}` sat unrendered in what would have gone to Vircle. The complementary guard (the AST brand-scanner) could not catch it either — it flags a LEFTOVER literal, not a BROKEN placeholder. Two transferable habits: (1) when a refactor's mechanical transform touches code beyond the snapshot's coverage, add a targeted static check for the transform's own failure signature (here, `grep -nE '\+ _[A-Z]+ \+ .[^{]*\{'` for a reopened plain segment carrying a placeholder) — the snapshot proves the covered set, the grep proves the rest; (2) a "works in both plain and f-strings" text substitution is a lie whenever the literal has an interpolation after the edit point — prefer producing clean f-strings for those occurrences and reserve the concat trick for edit points with nothing interpolated downstream. (Per-org branding, Sprint 5, 2026-07-24)

- **A test that ENUMERATES the thing it guards silently narrows every time the source grows — derive the list or assert it complete.** `manual.test.ts` keeps a hand-written `ROLE_CHAPTERS = ['role-reviewer', 'role-qc', 'role-org-admin', 'role-admin-general']` and asserts, among other things, that org_admin and super see all of them. Registering a fifth chapter left all 22 tests green while covering the new chapter with nothing at all: the array looks like the registry but is a COPY of it, so the suite quietly kept testing the old world. This is the same shape as the `_SUBJECT_BM`↔`subjects.ts` drift and the i18n scanner that a renamed helper turns into a no-op — already recorded twice in this file, but both prior instances were about a list mirroring ANOTHER FILE, and this one mirrors a list in the same module, which is why it didn't pattern-match. The general form: **whenever a test contains a literal collection of the things it is protecting, either derive it from the source (`CHAPTERS.filter(c => c.group === 'role').map(c => c.slug)`) or add an assertion that the literal is COMPLETE against the source** — otherwise the test's coverage is frozen at the moment it was written while its name goes on promising otherwise. Cheap tell during review: a test-local `const` array whose elements are ids/slugs/keys owned by production code. (Finance role, 2026-07-23)

- **Never conclude a thing DOESN'T EXIST from a search you truncated — and before scoping "build X", grep for X's own vocabulary.** Asked why a duplicate offer letter had not been archived, I grepped for writers of `superseded_at`, piped it through `head`, saw one hit in `income_engine`, and told the owner the supersede mechanism covered income proofs only and would need generalising — then scoped a sprint to build it.
