Edit-tool changes are respected; and always tsc after a bulk client-fn addition. (Contract Module S4, 2026-07-18)
- **Scope a single-key i18n rename by the exact value (assert it occurs once) or the full key path — never by "first `"<key>":` block".** `en.json` has TWO `verdict` blocks; a first-match block-scoped script overwrote the wrong one ("Interview scheduling") twice before revert. Safe pattern: `assert txt.count(f'"{old}"') == 1` then string-replace, or walk the full path. (Cockpit copy pass, 2026-07-21)
- **Check the LIVE env / scheduler before asserting a feature flag or cron cadence — memory notes rot.** I told the owner Vircle setup was "dark" and a sheet edit would persist; both were wrong (`VIRCLE_SETUP_ENABLED=1`; the relay sheet re-syncs every 15 min). `gcloud run services describe` and `gcloud scheduler jobs list` are the truth; the project memory is a hint that can be stale. (Vircle activation, 2026-07-21)
- **When backfilling a field, mirror the exact normalisation the live write-path applies.** A Vircle mobile backfill stored the raw `019-459 2358` while the Action Centre confirm path stores E.164 via `normalise_msisdn` (`+60…`); the owner spotted the inconsistency. Read the write-path first, reuse its normaliser. (Vircle activation, 2026-07-21)
- **Never characterise a document (or any record) from a TRUNCATED field value — re-select it in full first.** I told the owner two MySTR screenshots had been misclassified; #9 was not one of them. Its `doc_seen` had been cut off mid-string in a query result, and I drew a conclusion from the visible fragment — the full value read "…showing **Sumbangan Asas Rahmah** status", i.e. a SARA page the parser had classified correctly. A truncated value is a prompt to re-query, never evidence. (Cockpit + consent, 2026-07-23)
- **Before proposing to widen what counts as PROOF, ask how it could be forged — not just whether the inference is sound.** I proposed accepting a bank STR payment receipt because payment implies approval; the owner refused with the question I had skipped: *"We don't know if it is a typed doc."* A government portal screenshot has verifiable provenance; a receipt can be typed. For evidence changes, forgeability outranks logical validity. (Cockpit + consent, 2026-07-23)
- **A suite-level failure with ZERO failing tests is a crash — re-run before diagnosing.** Jest reported "3 failed, 37 passed" suites with 338 passed / 0 failed tests; a worker had died under memory pressure (8 GB box, `node_modules` junctioned from the main checkout). The clean re-run was 40/40, 632 tests. Suites-failed-but-no-test-failed means the runner fell over, not that the code regressed. (Cockpit + consent, 2026-07-23)
- **When backend enum values feed frontend i18n keys, ship a CLASS-covering parity guard with the first fix — never a per-value patch.** The same failure class hit twice (STR verdict copy rendered raw ICU 2026-07-01; missing `pathway_type_switch` key 2026-07-23) before `test_verdict_item_i18n.py` closed the whole verdict-item class: any new enum value now fails CI until its en/ms/ta keys exist. Apply the same pattern to any other enum→i18n surface the day it first breaks. (Consolidation review, 2026-07-23)
- **For an owner-facing feature, present the UI artifact BEFORE the first flag-on ask — not concurrently with it.** Sprint 15's `REQUESTS_ENABLED` flip-on ask was sequenced by "is prod smoke clean?" (a code-readiness gate); the owner's request to see the UI first arrived mid-turn, after the flag was already briefly ON (~2 min, caught and reverted). Code-level readiness and owner-readiness are different gates — a clean smoke test says nothing about whether the owner has seen what the feature looks like. This is distinct from the existing "prototype in Stitch before coding" rule (that gates the FIRST line of UI code); this gates the LIVE reveal of already-built UI. Sequence: build → smoke-test dark → show the UI artifact/mock → THEN ask to flip the flag. (Requests space, Sprint 15, 2026-07-24)
- **When you NARROW a gating/eligibility condition, re-read the user-facing copy that assumed the old, looser one.** The auto-nudge email hedged "complete any remaining steps" — written when the nudge could fire for a student with stray items — but the eligibility was later tightened to fire ONLY when `consent_blockers` is empty, so the copy promised work that can no longer exist and read like a nag. The owner caught it, not the tests (copy isn't asserted). A gating change and the copy that describes it are one edit. (Nudge + income route, 2026-07-24)
- **A "reconcile / fix-it-at-step-N" mechanism must be paired with proving the affected population can REACH step N.** The income-route auto-switch lived at consent, but a genuinely-STR student who picked "salary" was blocked by the salary gate BEFORE consent and never reached it, so the switch could never run. The owner spotted the gap ("does that mean the student is stuck?"). Whenever a fix lives at a downstream step, verify nothing upstream filters out the very cases it is meant to catch — the pairing fix here was the symmetric gate. (Nudge + income route, 2026-07-24)
- **A test pinned to a literal near-future calendar date rots the day the calendar catches up to it — freeze the clock instead of writing a "future" date as a literal.** Five payment-window tests from the 2026-07-22 sprint wrote owner-verified cases against dates like `date(2026, 7, 26)`, correct on the day they were written; on 2026-07-25 the real calendar reached that date and the tests silently stopped exercising their intended "this is in the future" edge case, breaking outright. The codebase already has a localdate-freeze convention elsewhere in the suite — it just wasn't applied here. When a test's correctness depends on "today" being before/after a specific date, freeze `today` explicitly rather than encoding the relationship into a literal that will eventually be wrong. (Billing & usage v1, Sprint 13a, 2026-07-25)
- When "what counts as X" (e.g. income evidence) is decided in more than one place — an assessment/verdict path AND a submission-gate path — they WILL drift; a permissive-looking UI option can feed the assessment while the gate still blocks. Give the rule ONE named predicate both sides call, and pin agreement with a test. (Sprint: Documents-tab kinder, 2026-07-25)
- **A visibility fence is not done when the API is fenced — enumerate every CHANNEL that surfaces the same data.** P3 scoped the sponsor pool's list and detail views, but the weekly digest and the real-time alert still selected from the unfenced queryset, so a Sabah-only funder would have learned BY EMAIL that flagship students exist. The root error was reasoning about "the pool" as an endpoint when the same rows reach the same audience through at least three channels (API, digest, real-time alert — and later exports, webhooks, admin CSVs). When adding a fence, list the channels first, fence them together, and add a **source guard** asserting each one narrows — so the next channel added fails CI until it is fenced too. (Platform P3, 2026-07-26)
- **Prefer REUSING an existing control chain over reimplementing one in the same domain — you inherit its emergent behaviour for free.** P4a's wallet credit calls the payments module's `finance_check_required()` rather than writing its own maker-checker. Two non-obvious behaviours came along unbidden and are now pinned by tests: appointing a finance admin **arms the check retroactively** for a record already mid-chain, and revoking the last one **degrades gracefully** back to a two-step chain mid-record. A bespoke implementation would have required inventing each case, and the retroactive one is easy to miss. Corollary: record a **currency rule** so the two chains cannot drift apart. (Platform P4a, 2026-07-26)
- **When a shared helper gains a scoping dimension that must never be defaulted, make the new parameter REQUIRED — never optional-with-a-safe-looking-default.** `sponsor_balance` became per-programme in P2a. `programme=None` as a default would have compiled, passed all 4593 existing tests, and silently pooled one gift's money with another's at every un-updated call site. Required-and-no-default turned the same mistake into an immediate `TypeError` at 13 sites — a complete, mechanical worklist instead of a silent wrong answer. Pair it with a **source guard** asserting the aggregate/unscoped variant (`sponsor_available_total`) is never consulted by an authorisation path, so a later edit cannot quietly reintroduce the pooled read while every number still looks plausible. (Platform P2a, 2026-07-26)
- **Do not edit source between launching a verification run and reporting its result.** Two import-tidying edits landed after a background full-suite run started in P2a; the reported "4604 green" had not actually covered them. The re-run passed, but trivial edits are exactly the category that hides regressions. Freeze → run → report; if an edit is needed, re-run. (Platform P2a, 2026-07-26)
- **Adding a TABLE to this Supabase-backed project means mirroring a sibling's RLS state and policy set — not just its column types.** The existing migrate-first lesson says to hand-write the Postgres DDL and copy types from `information_schema`; that is necessary but not sufficient. Also read `pg_class.relrowsecurity` and `pg_policies` for a neighbouring table: every HalaTuju app table runs RLS **enabled with exactly one** `Backend service role only` policy (`FOR ALL TO service_role USING (true) WITH CHECK (true)`). A new table created without it lands in the Supabase security advisor as `rls_enabled_no_policy` (or worse, RLS off entirely) and silently diverges from every neighbour — a dozen older tables already sit in that advisor list for exactly this reason. Check the PK form too: Django 5 renders `bigint GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY` here, not a serial. (Platform P1a programme layer, 2026-07-26)
- **"Unspendable" and "invisible" are two different guarantees — adding a state that means "not real yet" means enumerating every surface that DISPLAYS the thing, not just the one that spends it.** P4a gave donations a sign-off chain and proved the right thing about spending (`is_spendable`, tested four ways); nothing asserted visibility, so a `draft` credit — money nobody had signed off, possibly a typo — appeared on the sponsor's own giving statement as money we hold, and could conjure a wallet for a programme they had been given nothing in. Three surfaces read `sponsor.donations` unfiltered. This is the P3 channel lesson recurring one domain over (there: a fence on the pool API while the digest and real-time email still leaked; here: a status filter on the spend path while three display paths ignored it) — which is the point: the failure is not domain-specific, it is that a new state gets applied to the path you were thinking about. Fix shape is the same both times: ONE narrowing seam (`pool.for_sponsor`, `visible_donations`), every reader through it, and a source guard so the next surface fails CI until it narrows too. (Platform P4b, 2026-07-26)
- **Cost a "no schema change" claim against the DATA the change operates on, not against the existing column list.** P4b was scoped and communicated as migration-free, then needed three columns — because a correct distinctness rule keys on identity, and that only becomes obvious on reading the admin table and finding two ACTIVE accounts sharing the name "Ve. Elanjelian". The estimate was formed from the model file; the fact that invalidated it was one query away. Before promising a sprint is schema-free, look at the rows the new rule will run against. (Platform P4b, 2026-07-26)
- **In a multi-worktree checkout, a relative `docs/` path is ambiguous — use absolute paths for doc appends.** This repo has BOTH a root `docs/` (the live registers) and a legacy `halatuju_api/docs/`. The shell cwd was left at `halatuju_api` after a pytest run, so `cat >> docs/decisions.md` silently appended four platform decision records to the wrong, long-dormant register. Caught only because the next command couldn't find `docs/technical-debt.md`. Reverted with `git checkout HEAD --` and redone. Write doc appends with the full path, or `cd` to the repo root first in the same command. (Platform P4b, 2026-07-26)
- **A sprint brief written from the DATA MODEL outward will systematically miss the read paths — write the file list from "what displays this?" before starting.** P2b's brief said "add `PaymentRun.programme`; narrow `eligible_rows`". Correct and incomplete: the run-detail payload's *skipped this run* list computes live from the same choke-point, so narrowing only the item query would have left a student of another gift — never a candidate for that run — reading as "skipped by" it. This is the third consecutive sprint where enumerating channels found something the brief hadn't (P3: the digest and real-time emails; P4b: three sponsor-facing donation reads; P2b: the skipped list). Three in a row is not bad luck, it is the method: a brief authored from the schema lists what WRITES the data, and the bugs live in what READS it. Before coding, list every surface that renders the thing being changed, and put that list in the sprint plan. (Platform P2b, 2026-07-26)
- **"Nothing visible changes today" is not the same as low value — check WHY nothing changes.** P2b was scoped as reporting hygiene because a payment run currently spans one programme anyway. True, but only because one programme exists — the precise condition that ends the moment a second tenant or gift arrives, which is what the whole layer is being built for. The actual exposure was one benefactor's money paying another's students, on the live payout path. Sequencing it last was right (payments is live; the wallet work was Sabah's real dependency); calling it hygiene mis-priced it. When a change looks inert, ask whether it is inert by nature or inert only under today's data. (Platform P2b, 2026-07-26)
- **When a backend rule is RETIRED, grep for the frontend constant that mirrored it — a stale mirror is invisible until someone builds on it.** Every doc type became single-instance on 2026-06-05 (`_is_single_instance` → `True`, `MULTI_INSTANCE_DOC_TYPES` retired). The web app's copy of that list, `MULTI_INSTANCE_UPLOADS = {str, salary_slip, epf}`, survived — harmless for seven weeks, because nothing consulted it. Then the 2026-07-24 "tidier document rows" change used it to decide which cards get the tidy file row, and it silently exempted the two cards a B40 family looks at most (mother's STR, salary slip): Replace stranded in the header, filename bare below. The owner reported it as a styling bug; it was a data-flow belief two months out of date. Retiring a rule is not done when the enforcing code is deleted — it is done when every mirror of it is deleted too, or reduced to a form that cannot encode the retired distinction (here: a layout rule that takes only a file COUNT, so no doc type can be exempted). (Documents tab, 2026-07-26)
- **Size a cloud resource with `describe`, never the `list` column — and sanity-check the magnitude against the bill.** `gcloud artifacts repositories list` reported `cloud-run-source-deploy` as **"72.2 MB"**; `describe --format=json` reported **77,576 MB**. A thousandfold error, and the small number was the plausible one — 72 MB of images sounds right, so nothing prompts a second look. What caught it was arithmetic against the invoice: 72 MB cannot cost RM27/month at ~$0.10/GB. **When a reported size and a billed amount disagree by orders of magnitude, the size is wrong, not the bill.** (Billing cost review, 2026-07-26)
- **A cost model built from the metered surface will mis-weight the bill — read the actual invoice before deciding what to price.** `usage_events` was designed around the per-tenant variable costs (Gemini, Vision, email, WhatsApp) on the reasonable assumption that AI dominates. The June invoice says otherwise: the meter covers **RM7 of RM89 (8%)**; Cloud Run and Artifact Registry — neither metered — are 82%. The metering is not wrong, but precision there buys almost nothing, and a pricing sprint aimed at it would have polished the smallest line. Two related claims in the investigation doc were also stale (GCP "RM≈3–4/mo" vs RM89 actual; Supabase "free plan today" vs eight paid Pro invoices). **Before building anything that prices usage, reconcile the model against one real invoice per provider.** (Billing cost review, 2026-07-26)
- **A count the user asked for is not the same as a count that ADDS UP — reconcile before you render.** The owner asked a partner digest to carry five figures (applicants, rejected, shortlisted, awaiting review, awarded). Those five do not sum to the total: `submitted`, `interviewing`/`interviewed`/`recommended`, and `withdrawn`/`expired`/`closed` fall outside all of them, so a partner with 62 students would have been shown lines totalling 44 and drawn their own conclusion about the missing 18. Three more lines were added, and the test that matters walks EVERY value in `STATUS_CHOICES` and fails — naming it — if one is unclassified or claimed twice, so the next status added to the model cannot silently fall out of the email. The general rule: when a spec lists the buckets to show, check the buckets PARTITION the population, and pin the partition with a test rather than the buckets. (Partner comms S1, 2026-07-26)
- **When you propose an option to the owner, state the premise it rests on — or you will get approval for the wrong thing.** I offered "recipients = contact_email + the org's partner logins" and it was approved. The premise was that those logins were the partner's bursary representatives. They are not: both are course-selector accounts from March with no B40 scope at all, and sending them bursary progress would have disclosed applicant data to an audience attached to a different product. The owner caught it — *"You are confusing the Halatuju partners with BrightPath partners"* — and the decision had to be withdrawn after approval. A one-line premise beside each option ("assumes those logins are bursary contacts") would have surfaced it before the choice was made. Corollary: an approved decision resting on a false premise is not settled, it is mis-recorded — reverse it explicitly in decisions.md rather than quietly building the corrected version. (Partner comms S1, 2026-07-26)
- **`auto_now` is a system clock, not a user-activity clock — never surface it as one.** `updated_at` was the obvious source for a chase list's "last activity" column, and it would have been quietly wrong in the most damaging direction: verdict scoring, re-extraction, the institution sync and our own notification stamps all bump it, so a dormant student would have read as active this morning and the partner would have stopped chasing the very person who needed it. Anything a reader will interpret as "the user did something" must come from a field only the user's own action writes (here `ApplicantDocument.uploaded_at`), and the surface should say which action it measures. Pin it with a test that performs a SYSTEM-side `save()` and asserts the displayed date does not move. (Partner comms S1, 2026-07-26)
- **A sweep that re-checks state at SEND time is safer than an inline call at the transition — and it is also what lets you batch.** The partner milestone emails could have fired inline when an application reached `profile_complete` or `awarded`. Both transitions can be reverted (`revert_if_profile_incomplete`; `awarded → recommended` when an offer lapses), so an inline send would have told a partner organisation about a milestone that no longer existed, with no way to retract it. An hourly sweep whose queryset filters on the CURRENT status cannot do that: a reverted case simply never appears. Two further properties came free — several students arriving close together share one email instead of generating one each, and a send failure leaves the idempotency stamp unset so the next sweep retries. The general shape: for an outbound notification about a state that can change back, notify from a sweep over current state, not from the edge that entered it. (Partner comms S3, 2026-07-26)

- **A prefix-matching rule needs an explicit exception for the root of its own namespace.** The admin route registry resolves a path to its menu item by longest prefix, which is right for `/admin/payments/7 -> payments`. But `/admin` is a prefix of *every* admin URL and is an INDEX page whose siblings are separate routes, not its children — so an unrecognised path resolved to the dashboard and inherited its roles, which would have refused a reviewer on a page nobody had declared. One matching rule where the data needed two. The general form: whenever you write "longest prefix wins" over a route table, ask what the shortest entry is and whether it is a section or a page; a section owns its descendants, a page owns nothing. Marked `exact: true` and pinned by a test asserting an unknown route resolves to *undefined* rather than to anything. Cheap tell during review: a registry entry whose href is a strict prefix of every other entry. (Nav/IA N1, 2026-07-27)
- **A quoted test count is not a measured one — run the suite before it enters the record.** A parallel agent's handoff reported "3584 passed" at my HEAD against a recorded baseline of 4809; I drafted a retrospective quoting 3584 and caught it only because sprint-close step 10 mandates a full run across ALL test directories. The real figure was 4859 — theirs was a subset (`apps/scholarship` alone), which is a perfectly reasonable thing to run and a disastrous thing to quote. The repo was never wrong; the record nearly was. Two habits: (1) never carry another agent's (or your own earlier turn's) number into a durable document without re-measuring it; (2) when reporting a count, say what you ran — "3584 in apps/scholarship" is useful, "3584 passed" is a trap for whoever reads it next. (Nav/IA N1, 2026-07-27)
- **Consolidating a duplicate is a DELETION, so "tidy up later" is the wrong default.** Two agents independently wrote billing-rate tests in the same module; the other agent's handoff flagged the overlap and proposed consolidating after the deploy. On comparison their file covered every one of my assertions plus more, so the right move was to drop my 118 lines *before* shipping and port across the single assertion that was genuinely new. Deferring would have shipped two files asserting one contract — the exact failure mode already recorded here for duplicate *definitions* (a fix lands in one and not the other), which applies to duplicate *tests* just as hard, and worse: the stale one keeps passing while asserting the old contract. Ask which artefact is the superset; if yours is the subset, deleting it is lower risk than shipping it, not higher. (Nav/IA N1, 2026-07-27)
- **Do not chain the cleanup of a diagnostic artefact onto the command that produces it.** I ran a build redirected to a log and appended `rm -f` in the same compound command; the build failed, the log was already gone, and the nine-minute build had to be re-run on a box where builds are memory-constrained. The `rm` was wired to convenience rather than to the failure path. Delete a log after you have read it and acted on the result — never in the same breath as generating it. (Nav/IA N1, 2026-07-27)
- **An empty or old timestamp belongs to the object you QUERIED, not the one you are reasoning about.** I logged TD-178 as "the Artifact Registry cleanup policy is not reclaiming", citing surviving images dated 2026-03-14. Those were **package** `createTime` values; no *image* was that old in any actively-deployed package — the policy was enforcing to the second. The wrong cause reached two documents before anyone questioned it. Worse, when I corrected it I over-corrected, writing the March images off as imaginary; they were real, sitting in *dead* packages pinned by an unfiltered KEEP rule — a second, genuinely different cause. It took the owner asking an unrelated question ("would deleting these break Lentera?") to force the re-check that found both causes were partly true. General form: `X.createTime` answers a question about X and nothing else, and a correction is not finished until the ORIGINAL evidence has been re-examined rather than discarded. (Billing cost ledger, 2026-07-27)
- **Before modelling a money column, confirm the denomination of every source it will hold — not just the one in front of you.** `PlatformCost` shipped with a single `amount_myr`, generalised from GCP, which invoices in ringgit. The first real Supabase invoice was **USD**, and a same-day migration had to add `currency`/`amount_original`/`fx_rate`. One glance at an invoice would have caught it; instead the schema asserted something false about money and had to be widened under a live ledger. The corollary that saved it: make the converted column **nullable** so "we hold the invoice but do not yet know the ringgit" is representable, and report such a month as a floor. A placeholder figure in a money column gets quoted as if it were true. (Billing cost ledger, 2026-07-27)
- **Read a cloud bill by SKU before optimising anything — the service name hides which line is actually spending.** Artifact Registry (RM27) was investigated first purely because it was visible on the billing dashboard. The **larger** line, Cloud Run *Jobs* at RM33, only surfaced from a SKU-level BigQuery query — and the same service name, "Cloud Run", contains both the request-serving a tenant drives and the cron compute we drive. Classifying cost by service would have conflated them permanently. Measuring at the right grain was worth more than any optimisation that followed it. (Billing cost ledger, 2026-07-27)

- **When a review is blocked by infrastructure your sprint does not own, build the smallest thing that RENDERS the work rather than debugging the blocker.** Asked to click through a new admin shell, I sent the owner to the authenticated route and then spent three of their attempts on someone else's OAuth flow — wrong port, stale PKCE state, then a genuine exchange failure — before they had seen a single pixel. A 90-line throwaway page that mounted the real components with a stubbed identity got them there immediately, and I deleted it at close. The tell: the goal was *the components rendered*, and I had silently substituted *an authenticated session*, which is only the same thing if you assume the login must work. Ask what the review actually requires; a stub page, a story or a screenshot usually clears it, and the blocker can be logged as debt instead of chased mid-sprint. (Nav/IA N2, 2026-07-28)
- **Never run the test suite while someone is looking at a dev server on this box.** 8 GB does not hold a full jest run and a Next dev server at once: webpack died with `RangeError: Array buffer allocation failed` in the middle of the owner's review, and — worse — the dead process stayed bound to its port, so it read as alive to `netstat` and dead to the browser. This file already recorded that `next build` OOMs after a full suite; I applied that knowledge to builds and not to the identical case. Verification is never urgent enough to interrupt a person at the screen. (Nav/IA N2, 2026-07-28)
- **`TaskStop` kills the wrapper, not the process tree — confirm the port is free before believing a server is down.** Twice a stopped dev server kept serving: once quietly returning STALE code with the wrong API for a review, once holding the port so a fresh start failed. It surfaced only because a later start logged `EADDRINUSE` while `curl` still returned 200 — two facts that cannot both be true. After stopping any background server, check `netstat -ano | grep :PORT`, and kill the leftover BY PID (never by image name: `taskkill /im node.exe` takes out the MCP servers too). The general form: a success message from a supervisor is a claim about the supervisor, not about the child. (Nav/IA N2, 2026-07-28)
- **`cmd /c` silently does not execute from Git Bash — use PowerShell for Windows-native commands.** `mklink` and `taskkill` both printed the cmd banner, exited 0, and did nothing; a command that returns success without running is worse than one that fails. `powershell -NoProfile -Command` works — but bash expands `$` first, so a PowerShell variable in the command string gets eaten before PowerShell sees it (that cost a third attempt). Prefer a form with no `$`: fetch the value in bash (e.g. the PID from `netstat`) and pass it as a literal. (Nav/IA N2, 2026-07-28)

- **The moment a review is blocked by infrastructure your sprint does not own, BUILD THE PREVIEW — do not diagnose the blocker at all.** I wrote almost this lesson yesterday and still failed it today: asked for a browser pass, I sent the owner at a broken Google sign-in and burned four of their attempts across two sprints, diagnosing confidently and wrongly twice, before building the ninety-line stub page that showed the work immediately. The earlier phrasing ("build the smallest thing that renders the work") left me room to try the blocker "just once" first, and once became four. The trigger is not "after a reasonable attempt"; it is the FIRST failure. Log the blocker as debt in the same breath and move on. (Nav/IA N3b, 2026-07-28)
- **Surface the error before theorising about it — a generic failure message costs more than it hides.** The admin callback said "Authentication failed. Please try again." for three rounds of guessing while holding the exact reason: *PKCE code verifier not found in storage ... for SSR frameworks use @supabase/ssr to store it in cookies*. Rendering that string took minutes and ended the investigation on the first attempt. Before forming a hypothesis about a failure a screen already knows about, make the screen say it. Corollary for user-facing copy: "try again" is only honest when trying again could plausibly work. (Nav/IA N3b, 2026-07-28)
- **Asserting that a bulk edit MATCHED says nothing about whether the result reads correctly.** After adding assertions to every scripted replacement (last sprint's lesson), a Manual blurb still came out as "your team, your cases, sponsors and your team" — the substitution was right in isolation and wrong in context, and the assertion passed. Two further stale navigation paths survived the same pass because they did not contain the word being grepped for. After any bulk copy edit, READ the changed lines; and grep for what the copy MEANS ("Invite staff", "from the menu") as well as the term you are replacing. (Nav/IA N3b, 2026-07-28)

- **An unanswered question is not a defaulted one — and "what are we building" is never yours to default.** A preview went to the owner with five open questions and came back "looks good, proceed". I recorded all five as settled, in a decision table, including two about theming that were product scope rather than implementation detail. The correction was immediate once they saw it — *"Themes should be its own planning. Not just dark but other likely themes"* — and it was not merely procedural: planning around "dark mode" would have chosen a token set suited to one output, when the expensive part (naming 1,537 colours across 119 files) is identical for six themes or one. Approval of a direction is not approval of every choice inside it. Split the questions before assuming: the ones about HOW can take a stated default and be reversed cheaply; the ones about WHAT get left standing while you build the part that does not depend on them. (Nav/IA N4, 2026-07-28)
- **A placeholder for future work encodes an assumption about that work — if the assumption is not yours to make, do not leave the placeholder.** `uiPrefs.ts` shipped a `PREF_KEYS.theme` entry beside the one preference it actually had. It looked like tidiness; it silently asserted that a theme is a *device* preference rather than a person's or a tenant's, which was precisely the open question. A later sprint would have found the key sitting there and read it as decided — the cheapest possible way to launder a guess into a fact. Reserved slots in a menu are the legitimate version of this pattern, and the difference is instructive: those were owner-approved, render visibly as "soon", and have a test forcing the flag off when the page appears. An invisible constant has none of that. (Nav/IA N4, 2026-07-28)
- **Grep the copy for what it MEANS, not the words you are replacing — the third time this has found something.** N4's Manual pass turned up *"The links along the top are your workspace"*, false since N2 moved them into a sidebar, and it survived both intervening currency passes because it contains neither "Administration" nor "sidebar". Beside it, a self-contradicting FAQ answer — *"It's in the sidebar … Payments card. There is no separate menu entry for it"* — each half true at a different point in the same fortnight. A term-based grep finds copy that NAMES the thing you changed; it cannot find copy that DESCRIBES it. Search for the shape ("along the top", "on the left", "card", "tab") as well as the noun. (Nav/IA N4, 2026-07-28)
- **A preview is not just a fallback for a blocked review — it is a better starting artefact than a written spec.** The standing lesson said to build a preview the moment a review is blocked. N4 started there instead: the owner approved an interactive mock-up rather than a plan, which settled the open/close behaviour, the dot-versus-number badge and the collapsed group boundary by looking rather than by describing. All three would otherwise have been prose nobody could evaluate. On a project where local admin sign-in has been broken for three sprints (TD-182), the preview is the review surface anyway — so building it first costs nothing and buys the decisions early. (Nav/IA N4, 2026-07-28)
- **Do not let a styling class be a test's selector.** A rail assertion matched `[aria-hidden].rounded-full` to find a notification dot, which equally described the icon beside it. The fix was a `data-badge` hook: a class expresses how a thing looks and is free to change, while a data attribute is a contract a test may hold. Same instinct as never asserting on generated class names — if a test needs to find an element, give it something meant to be found. (Nav/IA N4, 2026-07-28)

- **In a repo where two agents work in parallel, a monotonically-increasing ID is not safe to allocate by reading the file — re-verify it at CLOSE.** The nav agent and the sponsor agent both appended to `docs/technical-debt.md` from separate worktrees on the same day, each reading the register, each taking the next two numbers. Both got TD-185 and TD-186. The merge resolved cleanly because the additions were at different offsets, so git never saw a conflict and the collision passed straight into main — into seven referencing documents, memory and Mission Control. It surfaced only because sprint-close step 4 walks the register. The habit that fixes it costs one command: allocate the number when you write the row, then at close run `grep -o "TD-1[0-9][0-9]" docs/technical-debt.md | sort -u | tail -1` and confirm yours is still unique, because by then the other agent's merge has landed. Generalises to anything hand-numbered — migrations are safe only because Django refuses duplicates. (Nav/IA arc close, 2026-07-28)
- **A web sprint in this repo costs its feature files plus about a third again in copy — estimate accordingly.** N3b was planned at 10 files and touched 17; N4 was described as ~12 and touched 24. Both undercounts came from listing what holds the FEATURE and forgetting what DESCRIBES it: the Manual chapters, the FAQ, three i18n locales, the screenshot manifest. That ripple is not incidental here — the Manual/FAQ currency rule guarantees it, and the rule earns its keep (it found something false in three of the arc's four sprints). Count the copy when you scope, or every estimate is short in the same direction. (Nav/IA arc close, 2026-07-28)
- **Debt logged with the CONDITION that ends it gets closed; debt logged with a GUESS about its cause rots.** One arc, two clean examples. TD-181 was written the moment it was created, naming the sprint that would delete it — and that sprint deleted it, one for one. TD-187 was logged the same way, with the trigger stated ("a menu past ~20 rows"). Against them, TD-182 has survived three sprints: it was logged as a symptom with a hypothesis attached, the hypothesis was wrong twice, and each wrong version was read by the next session as established fact. Write the row as "this matters when X" or "this dies in sprint Y", never as "this is probably caused by Z". (Nav/IA arc close, 2026-07-28)

- **A trigger is a claim about DATA — run the query before you write it into a document.** I parked N3a against "more than one active `PartnerOrganisation` exists", reasoning from the model: that table is described as the tenant fence, so more than one row must mean more than one tenant. Production has had **ten** for months — nine are REFERRAL organisations (schools, NGOs) and only BrightPath owns a cohort or programme. The table holds both kinds with no flag between them, which its own docstrings say and which I had quoted at another agent the same day. So the condition I wrote to un-park future work had already fired the moment I wrote it, in four durable documents. One `SELECT` caught it the next morning. The general form: a schema tells you what CAN exist, never what DOES; any sentence of the form "when there is more than one X" is a query, not an inference. (PF-1, 2026-07-28)
- **When adding to an existing docstring, anchor the edit INSIDE it — never on the code that follows.** I appended prose plus a closing `"""` to a view class by anchoring on `permission_classes = [AllowAny]`, forgetting the docstring already had a closer. Two closers, so the new prose parsed as code: `SyntaxError: invalid character '—'`, every test in the module erroring at import. Trivial to fix and caught in seconds because the tests ran straight after — which is the actual lesson: the cost of this mistake is bounded entirely by how soon you run something. (PF-1, 2026-07-28)
- **A git worktree shares history, not `node_modules` — and "backend-only" stops being true the moment the owner answers a question.** I created the worktree for a backend sprint, then the routing answer pulled the frontend in, and the first `jest` run failed with `Module ts-jest ... not found`. A PowerShell junction to the main checkout's `node_modules` fixes it in seconds with no disk cost (`New-Item -ItemType Junction`), and is worth doing at worktree creation rather than at first failure, because the scope of a sprint is not knowable while a product question is still open. (PF-1, 2026-07-28)
- **Prove a "nothing changes today" claim with the tests that DIDN'T move, not with a new one.** PF-1's safety half was shippable ahead of the product decision only if single-tenant behaviour was byte-identical. The evidence is not the fifteen new tests — it is that 3,662 existing ones passed untouched. A new test proves the new behaviour; only the old suite passing unmodified proves the old behaviour survived. State it that way in the commit, because "3677 passed" alone reads as "I added tests" rather than "I changed nothing you rely on". (PF-1, 2026-07-28)

- **A correctly-built responsive component renders TWO copies of its controls — so every singular `getBy*` query against one is wrong.** `getByRole('button', {name: 'admin.next'})` threw *found multiple elements* on the shared `Pagination` footer, which draws a mobile and a desktop copy, both in the DOM, one hidden by CSS. That is not a bug to route around; it is what responsive markup looks like, and it means the assertion was written from an imagined render rather than a read one. Two more in the same sitting from the same cause: `toBeDisabled` is not a matcher this project loads (use `.disabled`), and the template switch answers to `role="switch"`, not `button`. Read the component's JSX before asserting on it, expect `getAllBy*` on anything shared, and where an element MUST be findable give it a `data-` hook — the same conclusion the nav sprint reached from the other side (a styling class is not a selector). (Sponsor tables, 2026-07-28)
- **A test count is a measurement of a TREE, so it is only true if it was taken on the tree that shipped.** The sorting CHANGELOG entry said 1026 jest; the merged tree runs 1032. Nothing was wrong when written — the concurrent PF-1 merge landed afterwards and moved the number under a document that had already been committed. Counts belong at sprint CLOSE, measured on the final merged tree, never captured while the prose is being written. This is the general shape of the day's other numeric corrections too (a revision ID, an env-var value, a TD number): in a repo two agents are pushing to, any figure written mid-sprint is a claim with a short shelf life, and the close is the only point at which it can be honest. (Sponsor tables, 2026-07-28)
- **"Make it look like that other screen" is a pointer to existing CODE, not a design brief.** The owner asked for the applications-list paging footer on four sponsor tables, with an image; I started reasoning about the control before searching for it, and `components/Pagination` plus `docs/partner-pagination-plan.md` had been built for exactly that footer weeks earlier. A search caught it in minutes, but the instinct was backwards, and the same request phrasing has now appeared three times in this project (the Sources badge pair, the pool sort/filter, this). When a request names another screen, go read that screen first — the answer to "how should this behave" is usually already committed, tested, and approved. (Sponsor tables, 2026-07-28)
- **When a guard exists so that FUTURE call sites are safe, put it where they cannot miss it — an exception, not a return value.** `_current_application()` feeds thirteen endpoints. Making it return a sentinel would have needed a `try/except` (or an `if`) at each, and the failure mode of forgetting one is a 500 or, worse, the old silent behaviour — while the entire purpose of the change is that a FOURTEENTH endpoint cannot reintroduce the bug. A DRF `APIException` propagates to the framework's handler, so every existing and future call site gets the right 409 without knowing the class exists. This cut against the module's house style (explicit `Response(..., status=...)` everywhere), and the house style was the wrong instinct here: consistency is worth less than a guard that cannot be forgotten. Ask which failure you are optimising against — a reader's surprise, or a future author's omission. (Multi-programme M1, 2026-07-28)
- **A brand-literal guard will catch your copy, and the fix is usually a token that already exists.** New i18n copy hardcoded `help@halatuju.xyz`; `brand-guard.test.ts` failed on all three locales. The reflex is to allowlist it. The right move was one grep: `{supportEmail}` is one of five branding tokens `t()` already auto-injects, used by neighbouring strings for exactly this. Every "the guard is in my way" moment on a mature codebase is worth thirty seconds of looking for the sanctioned mechanism first — the guard usually exists BECAUSE the mechanism does. (Multi-programme M1, 2026-07-28)
- **A suite that fails in the full run and passes alone is the box, not the code — but re-run the FULL suite to prove it.** `AwardComprehensionQuiz.test.tsx` reported "Test suite failed to run" in a 67-suite run and passed in isolation seconds later; a clean re-run of everything was green. On this 8 GB machine that is the documented worker-exhaustion transient, already recorded for `next build`. The discipline: isolation passing is a HINT, not proof — it changes the number of workers. Only a green full re-run rules out an interaction, and it costs twenty seconds. (Multi-programme M1, 2026-07-28)

- **"There is no X" is a QUERY, and grepping the code is not running it — this is the second failure of the same lesson in one day.** Asked whether sponsors accept any terms, I searched the i18n copy and the Python for undertakings, found none, and told the owner flatly that none exist anywhere. They do: `contract_templates` id 9 holds 94 lawyer-drafted clauses with a `Donor Obligations` section and 11 quiz checkpoints. It was invisible to every search I ran because **authored content lives in the DATABASE, not in the repo** — the contract module was built precisely so clauses stop being code. The PF-1 lesson said a schema tells you what CAN exist and never what DOES; the sharper form is: in a system with ANY authoring surface, the repo is not the corpus. Before asserting absence, ask what table a human would have typed it into. (Sponsor terms enquiry, 2026-07-28)
- **An INVISIBLE placeholder has no mechanism to end it; a VISIBLE one does.** `programmeName={undefined}` sat hardcoded in `AppShell` for four sprints and was found by the owner comparing the built console with the approved design — not by a test, a lint or a ticket. In the same arc, the reserved nav slots waited just as long and all got filled, because they render on screen as "soon" and a test forces the flag off the moment their page exists. Same intent, opposite outcomes, and the difference is entirely whether the gap can be SEEN. The rule is not "avoid placeholders" — it is that a placeholder needs a mechanism that ends it: a visible state, a failing test, or a dated ticket. A quiet `undefined` has none of the three and will outlive everyone's memory of it. (Nav/IA N3a, 2026-07-28)
- **Read the file you are appending to before writing the call — pattern memory is not knowledge of THIS codebase.** I wrote a new API client using `adminRequest` and `AdminApiOptions`; the module uses `adminFetch` and `ApiOptions`. `tsc` caught it in seconds so the cost was nil, but it is the same failure as quoting a test count without measuring: asserting a fact about the repository from memory rather than from the repository. One `grep` of the file's existing exports would have been faster than the round trip. (Nav/IA N3a, 2026-07-28)
- **A `node_modules` junction into a git worktree breaks `next build`'s standalone file-tracing — the compile is still valid.** The junction (from M1's lesson) saves a 400 MB install and lets jest run immediately, but the standalone output step then tries to symlink through it and fails with `EPERM`. `Compiled successfully` and `Generating static pages (66/66)` both pass, which is the type-check and the build; only the post-build trace copy fails, and only locally — Cloud Build has a real `node_modules`. Worth knowing so the warning is not mistaken for a broken build, and worth NOT "fixing" by dropping the junction. (Nav/IA N3a, 2026-07-28)

- **A test written from the same misunderstanding as the code will PASS and cement the bug.** I shipped an org switcher that listed every `PartnerOrganisation`, and wrote `test_super_sees_every_organisation_and_programme` to cover it. Green, and wrong: production's table holds ten rows of which one is a tenant and nine are REFERRAL organisations, so a super's switcher offered schools and NGOs as tenants. The owner found it in a screenshot. Tests only catch what you did not already believe — coverage is no defence against a wrong premise, because the assertion is written by the same person who held it. The countermeasure is not more tests: it is to write at least one test from the DATA rather than from the intent ("what does production actually contain, and would this list it?"), which here would have failed instantly. (Nav/IA N3a fix, 2026-07-28)
- **Writing a trap down does not stop you walking into it — the note has to sit where the mistake is made.** During PF-1, hours earlier, I discovered that `partner_organisations` holds funders and referrers with no flag between them, verified it against production, and wrote it into memory in bold: *"Never infer a tenant count from that table."* I then wrote `PartnerOrganisation.objects.filter(is_active=True)` in the next sprint. The memory note was in the right file and useless, because nothing surfaced it at the moment of writing that queryset. What would have worked is a comment on the MODEL, or a manager method (`PartnerOrganisation.objects.tenants()`) that makes the safe form the obvious one. Prose in a knowledge base is a reminder for someone already asking the question; a manager, a docstring on the field, or a required argument is a reminder for someone who is not. (Nav/IA N3a fix, 2026-07-28)

- **When a display looks wrong, ask what the DATA says before changing the view.** The console breadcrumb read "BrightPath Bursary / BrightPath Bursary", so I suppressed the duplicate — a display fix for what was actually a misnamed row: the ORGANISATION had been seeded with the PROGRAMME's name. The suppression was worse than useless, because a repeated name is a *signal* that something is misnamed and I had just deleted the signal. Same shape as the standing "fix the system, not the case" rule, pointed at rendering: a view faithfully showing bad data is not a view bug. Ask "is this string wrong, or is the thing it names wrong?" before touching the component. (N3a follow-up, 2026-07-28)
- **A breadcrumb answers "where am I", so it must be derived from the current route — not from what exists.** The first cut rendered the organisation and programme crumbs on every page, because it was built from the scopes ENDPOINT (what you may look at) rather than from the current page. The owner spotted it immediately from three screenshots: a platform page is outside any organisation and should show HalaTuju alone. The fix needed no new data — `NavItem.scope` already existed and is what groups the sidebar — but it needed the right QUESTION. When a component takes a list and a selection, check whether the thing it renders is a property of the list or of the current location. (N3a follow-up, 2026-07-28)
- **A failure message is a hypothesis from someone who cannot see your setup — treat it as a lead, not a diagnosis.** TD-182 sat open for weeks behind Supabase's own words: *"PKCE code verifier not found in storage ... For SSR frameworks (Next.js, SvelteKit, etc.), use `@supabase/ssr` ... to store the code verifier in cookies."* That sentence is authoritative-sounding, specific, names our exact framework — and was wrong for our failure. The real cause was that sign-in began at `localhost:3000` and returned at `127.0.0.1:3000`; browser storage is per-ORIGIN, so cookies would have been just as absent. Following the advice would have migrated the auth storage layer of every admin and sponsor login to fix nothing. The library was reasoning about the case it sees most often; it had no idea our two ends were different origins. Ask what the message CANNOT know about your setup before acting on it. (TD-182, 2026-07-28)
- **Reproduce with the smallest instrument that can distinguish the hypotheses — often you do not need the credentials the flow appears to demand.** This looked like it needed a real Google sign-in to investigate, which is why three prior attempts theorised instead. It did not: clicking the Google button writes the PKCE verifier BEFORE leaving for Google, so the whole question — does storage survive from login to callback — was answerable by clicking once, navigating straight to the callback, and reading `localStorage` on each host. Two minutes, and it produced a table that killed the accepted remedy outright. Before concluding a bug is not locally reproducible, ask which *step* actually writes the state you are reasoning about; it is frequently earlier than the step that needs the credentials. (TD-182, 2026-07-28)
- **When a probe cannot fail, it has told you nothing — always run the negative control.** Checking whether `localhost:3000` was in Supabase's redirect allowlist, the first probe (`/auth/v1/authorize`) echoed it back happily, which read as confirmation. Passing `https://evil.example.com` echoed back happily too: that endpoint defers validation, so the probe could not distinguish allowed from forbidden. A second endpoint (`/auth/v1/verify`) honoured both real origins and rewrote the bogus one to the site URL — same question, and this time an answer worth having. Any time a check returns the result you expected, spend one call proving it would have returned something else otherwise. (TD-182, 2026-07-28)
- **Guard the entry to a multi-origin flow, never the exit.** The canonical-host redirect belongs on the login pages: force sign-in to START on `localhost`, and since `redirectTo` is built from the initiating origin, it must FINISH there too. The same redirect on the callback would be a bug — a sign-in that legitimately began on `127.0.0.1` also returns there and works, and bouncing it to `localhost` would strand it from the verifier it was about to use, manufacturing the very failure. The general shape: when a flow carries state across a round trip, normalise the state at the point it is CREATED; normalising at the point it is CONSUMED destroys the correct cases along with the broken ones. (TD-182, 2026-07-28)
- **A confident wrong explanation is worse than the vague one it replaced — and a diagnostic that CAN be asked at the wrong moment will be.** Having just proved that the OAuth failure was an origin split, I shipped a message saying so, and the owner hit it within the hour on the very host his sign-in had started from. The check read "is the verifier missing?" AFTER `getSession()`, which awaits client init, which runs `detectSessionInUrl`, which deletes the verifier once it has POSTed — success or failure alike. After that line, "never here" and "here a moment ago" are the same observation, so every unrelated exchange failure became an accusation about the address bar. The function was correct; the call site was not. Two things generalise: (1) when a diagnostic reads mutable state, its VALIDITY WINDOW is part of its contract — put it in the name (`…AtEntry`) rather than a comment, because the next caller will not read the comment; (2) a diagnostic should EXPLAIN a failure that has happened, never PREDICT one — take the reading early, carry it, and apply it only once something has actually gone wrong. (TD-182 follow-up, 2026-07-28)
- **Fixing the blocker is not the same as unblocking the work — check the whole path before telling someone it is clear.** TD-182 had blocked local console review for six sprints; I fixed it, verified sign-in end-to-end, and sent the owner to `localhost:3000/admin/login`. It could never have worked: `.env.local` points the console at `http://localhost:8000` (nothing listening) and the live API's `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` lists only `halatuju.xyz` and the two Cloud Run hosts, so the callback's role check dies either way. I had fixed the step I was looking at and announced the outcome I wanted. Before declaring a workflow unblocked, walk it to the END — every hop it depends on, not just the one you repaired. (TD-182 follow-up, 2026-07-28)

- **`next build` on a WARM `.next` cache is not the gate you think it is — CI builds cold and will find what you missed.** T2's local build exited 0; the Cloud Build for the same commit failed on a type error in the file I had just edited (`SponsorTermsCard`'s `t` prop was typed `Record<string, string | number>` while the app's real `t` takes `Record<string, string>`). An incremental build does not necessarily re-typecheck a file whose dependency graph it believes is unchanged, and I had run that build after a dozen other edits, so the cache was thoroughly warm. The result was a half-deploy: web shipped, api did not, and I had already told the owner the gates were green. **Run `npx tsc --noEmit` and grep it for the files you actually touched** — it is seconds, it is cold by construction, and it catches precisely this class. Treat a passing warm `next build` as evidence the app still bundles, not as evidence your new code typechecks. (Sponsor terms T2, 2026-07-28)
- **A page nested under a global provider inherits everything that provider does — including the things you excluded it for.** The design sandbox was built with its own provider stack that deliberately omits `AuthProvider`, because that provider mints an ANONYMOUS Supabase user on mount and a sandbox handed to an outside design team must not create real auth rows. It minted them anyway: the sandbox layout nests inside the ROOT layout, whose `<Providers>` still mounted the real `AuthProvider` above it. Writing a clean stack for your subtree does not remove what sits above it — the exclusion has to happen where the provider itself decides, which here was an existing path predicate. Found by reading the browser console (two 401s on `/auth/v1/signup`), not by any test, on a screen whose whole purpose is to touch nothing real. **When a component must not run somewhere, gate it at the component, not by declining to mount it in your own tree.** (Config Layer 0 sprint 1, 2026-07-28)
- **Two rules with the same effect and different reasons should stay two rules.** The sandbox needed the student auth stack inert, which `isPrivilegedConsolePath()` already achieves for `/admin` and `/sponsor`. Adding `/sandbox` to that list would have been one line and wrong: the predicate is read elsewhere as a statement about privileged consoles, and a sandbox is not one. It became `isAnonymousAuthSuppressed()`, which composes the console rule with the sandbox rule and says why each exists. The cost of merging them is not the mistake today — it is the reader in six months who trusts what the name asserts. Same family as the 2026-07-15 surface-partition incident, where a display rule and a security rule were confused. (Config Layer 0 sprint 1, 2026-07-28)
- **Compile a thing out rather than hiding it at runtime, when "it must not be reachable" is the requirement.** The sandbox route could have shipped everywhere and called `notFound()` on an env check — one line, and it would have put the fixtures and the stubbed `fetch` into every production visitor's bundle. Naming its pages `page.sandbox.tsx` and gating that extension in `next.config.js` `pageExtensions` means an ordinary build contains no route and no sandbox code at all, verified by grepping the build's own route table. A runtime guard answers "can they see it"; a build-time one answers "is it there". (Config Layer 0 sprint 1, 2026-07-28)

- **When two builds fire for one commit, read the SERVICE NAME off each — the list order means nothing.** T2's web build failed and its api build passed; I inferred the opposite from the order `gcloud builds list` returned them in, and told the owner production was in the worse of the two states. The correction was one `--format="value(substitutions._SERVICE_NAME)"` away and I had not run it. Generalises past Cloud Build: when a tool returns a list of similar things and you need to know WHICH, the ordering is never the answer — ask for the discriminating field. Reporting a production state wrongly during an incident of your own making costs more trust than the incident. (Sponsor terms arc, 2026-07-28)
- **A loader must never clear error state.** Writing the 409 test for the sponsor-terms wizard exposed that `load()` began with `setError('')`, so the "these terms changed while you were reading" message was wiped a millisecond after being set — the sponsor would have been bounced to the top of a document with no explanation whatsoever. Clear-on-fetch feels tidy and is wrong: an error explains an ACTION the user took, and a refetch is not one. Clear on the next action, never in the loader. The test caught it only because it asserted the MESSAGE was visible rather than that the phase had reset — assert on what the user would see, not on the state machine. (Sponsor terms arc, 2026-07-28)
- **A tab in a layout spec is not a statement about where authoring happens.** The owner asked for "Clauses, Quiz, Preview and Deploy"; I read the Quiz tab as "the quiz is edited there" and moved the checkpoint away from the clause it tests, which they had liked where it was. The resolution was better than either of my versions — write the quiz inline, and let the Quiz tab TAKE the quiz — and it only appeared once I asked what each surface was FOR rather than which instruction was most recent. When a spec and an earlier working design conflict, that is the question. (Sponsor terms arc, 2026-07-28)
- **A default labelled "recommended for simplicity" can be the one option that cannot work for you — read what it assumes, not what it recommends.** Enabling IAP on the sandbox's Cloud Run service left it on **Google-managed OAuth**, the pre-selected default. Every request then returned a bare `502` for an hour of investigation. The console's own description said the condition plainly — Google-managed is *"best suited for apps for users within your organisation"* — and HalaTuju has no organisation, so Google could not provision the client it promises to manage. The answer was the second radio button, **Custom OAuth**, described as being for *"external users … allowlisting"*. Two CLI dead ends on the way: `gcloud iap oauth-brands create` refuses with *"Project must belong to an organization"* (and those APIs were shut down in March 2026), and `gcloud beta run services update` has no flag to attach a client — the step is console-only. **Recipe kept in `docs/plans/2026-07-28-configuration-layers-roadmap.md`.** (Sandbox deploy, 2026-07-28)
- **When a managed service fails opaquely, read the RESPONSE HEADERS AND BODY before reading the logs.** The Cloud Run logs showed a healthy container ("Ready in 48.5s") and nothing else, because the failure was entirely in front of it. `curl -D -` gave the answer in one line — `x-goog-iap-generated-response: true` identified WHO was failing, and a 50-byte body named WHAT. Same shape as the TD-182 lesson from earlier the same day (*"surface the error before theorising about it"*), and the same corollary: an infrastructure layer that intercepts a request will usually tell you why, but never in the logs of the thing behind it. (Sandbox deploy, 2026-07-28)
- **A comment asking two files to stay in step is a request; only a test is a rule.** The Layer 0 seam holds a fallback list of what BrightPath asks for, and the seed command holds the same list again — two descriptions of one truth, in different files. The first draft's fallback omitted `consent`, so an application WITH a programme would have been gated on it and a legacy application WITHOUT one would not — and consent is a legal requirement, not a programme preference. It would have failed silently, on exactly the rows nobody looks at. My first fix was to add the missing entry and write *"these describe the same thing and must not be written independently"* above it — while leaving them written independently. The real fix was three lines asserting that the fallback tuples equal what the seeded catalogue resolves to. **When you find yourself writing "keep these in sync", you have identified a test, not a comment.** (Config Layer 0 sprint 2, 2026-07-28)
- **Write the seed before trusting the schema — it is the first moment the model meets real values.** `ApplicationItem.default_state` shipped its first draft as `default_on: bool`. Four documents are *offered but never blocking* today (water bill, electricity bill, statement of intent, photo), and a boolean cannot express that. A flag that cannot represent a state the system ALREADY HAS is a schema asserting something false. It surfaced the moment I began enumerating real items, while both tables were still empty, so the correction was a drop-and-add instead of a migration under live rows. Same shape as the billing ledger's `amount_myr` (one glance at a real invoice would have shown USD): **enumerate the actual values a column must hold before you fix its type.** (Config Layer 0 sprint 2, 2026-07-28)
- **When a sprint's claim is "nothing changed", the evidence is the tests you did NOT touch.** Layer 0 sprint 2 added a catalogue, a selection table and a read seam, and deliberately left the literals it will replace live in `services.py`. That is what made the claim checkable: both descriptions existed at once and a test asserted they agreed, while the other ~5000 tests passed with no edits. Had the seam and the switchover shipped together, the sprint introducing the abstraction would also have been the sprint changing behaviour, with nothing to diff against. **Split "build the seam" from "move the callers" whenever the callers are load-bearing** — the intermediate state feels redundant and is the only place the equivalence can be proven. (Config Layer 0 sprint 2, 2026-07-28)
- **A green suite proves nothing about a dependency no fixture exercises — and "all tests pass" can be the most dangerous sentence in a sprint.** Wiring the submission gates to the Layer 0 catalogue, all 5018 backend tests passed on the first run. They passed because no fixture seeds the catalogue, so every test took the no-programme fallback branch and none took the branch that had just been written. Meanwhile production carries 143 applications, **every one with a programme**, against catalogue tables that were still empty — so `required_documents()` returned `{}`, `documents_done` became `set().issubset(present)` (vacuously true), and all 60 students inside the submission gate could have submitted with no documents at all. The suite would have stayed green through the incident. **When a change introduces a new dependency, ask which branch the existing tests take — if they all take the old one, green is measuring the code you did not write.** The fix that mattered was not the guard; it was writing a test from the shape of PRODUCTION (programme set, catalogue empty) rather than from what the code was meant to do, and then disabling the guard to watch it fail. (Config Layer 0 sprint 3a, 2026-07-29)
- **Absence of configuration must mean "not configured", never "requires nothing".** The resolver treated "this application has a programme" as licence to trust the catalogue, so an empty catalogue resolved to an empty requirement set — and an empty requirement set opens every gate that asks `required.issubset(present)`. The safe default for a permission-shaped question is the OLD behaviour, not the empty one: when a configuration source has nothing to say, fall back to what the system did before it existed. This is the same shape as the earlier "no programme → platform defaults" branch, which I had reasoned about carefully and then failed to extend to the empty-catalogue case one line away. **Every branch that can produce an empty answer to "what is required?" needs the same question asked of it.** (Config Layer 0 sprint 3a, 2026-07-29)
- **Deferring a data step is a decision that expires — re-examine it when the code around it changes.** Sprint 2 deliberately left the production catalogue unseeded, reasoning correctly that nothing read it, so empty was exactly as inert as seeded. Sprint 3a made the gates read it, and that reasoning silently became false: from that moment an empty catalogue was a state the system had an opinion about. The deferral was right when made and wrong when the dependency landed, and nothing in the process re-opened it — it took finding the bug. **When a sprint starts consuming something a previous sprint deferred, the deferral is part of the change's blast radius.** (Config Layer 0 sprint 3a, 2026-07-29)
- **When a sprint changes what a list is USED for, everything that list ever omitted becomes the blast radius — a list nobody reads is never wrong.** Layer 0's catalogue and the web app's `OTHER_OPTIONAL_DOC_TYPES` both claimed to describe the optional documents, and both omitted `school_leaving_cert`, which the Documents tab had been rendering for months. The union `DocType` omitted it too; it compiled only because the card helper takes a bare `string`. Nothing caught it because until sprint 3b nothing consulted those lists to decide what to DRAW — they were descriptions with no consumer, and a description with no consumer cannot be contradicted. The moment the catalogue started governing the render, that omission would have silently withdrawn a document students can upload today. This is the general form of the "a consumed deferral is in the blast radius" rule from 3a: **the trigger is not only deferred WORK, it is any list, constant or config that acquires a new reader.** Before wiring a source of truth into a new consumer, diff it against the thing it now claims to govern — here, the JSX. (Config Layer 0 sprint 3b, 2026-07-29)
- **A test that asserts a value MOVED must also assert something comparable did NOT — otherwise it cannot distinguish "this moved" from "everything moved".** The first cut of "a programme promoting the water bill to required reaches the student" asserted `water_bill` was in `required` and absent from `optional`. Both hold when EVERY document is reported required — a real way to get a resolver wrong, and one that renders as a wall of red asterisks. It passed the bite-check (the deliberate break) for exactly that reason, which is what exposed it; review had not. Adding one line about the untouched `electricity_bill` made it discriminate. The same shape applies to any filter, projection or classification: **pin the negative case in the same test, not a different one.** (Config Layer 0 sprint 3b, 2026-07-29)
- **A docstring that describes THE CURRENT SPRINT has a shelf life — write status as a dated sequence, not as a claim about now.** `requirements.py` opened with "⚠ THIS SPRINT IS DELIBERATELY INERT. Nothing calls these functions to make a decision yet", which was true when written and false one sprint later once 3a wired the gates — and nothing in the sprint that falsified it went back to say so. A reader arriving cold would have believed the loudest warning in the file. The fix is a form, not a reminder: "Sprint 2 landed this inert, 3a moved the document gates onto it, 3b put it on the payload" cannot rot, because it never asserted a present tense. (Config Layer 0 sprint 3b, 2026-07-29)
- **A colour scan that reads only class names misses the colour in inline styles, SVG fills and lib constants — and those are exactly the ones that stay light when everything around them inverts.** Layer 1 F1 converted the sponsor portal, and a test asserting "no raw Tailwind colour remains" passed. The giving donut is a `conic-gradient`, which cannot be expressed as a utility, so its three colours sat as `#2563eb` / `#22c55e` / `#e5e7eb` in an inline `style` — invisible to the scan, and a light-mode island in dark. The same hiding places recur: `courseBadges.ts`, `applicationStatus.ts`, `requestStatus.ts` and `paymentStatus.ts` return Tailwind classes as STRINGS from TypeScript, so a codemod over `.tsx` never sees them either. **When migrating a visual system, enumerate the ways the old thing can be spelled before believing a scan that reports zero.** The guard now also refuses a bare hex, with a documented allowlist for Google's logo — a third-party mark must not follow our theme. (Layer 1 F1, 2026-07-29)
- **A codemod can rename a colour correctly and classify it wrongly — the mechanical pass is where the SEMANTIC question surfaces, not where it is answered.** Renaming `blue` to `info` across the sponsor portal was right in every individual case and wrong about the page: the primary call-to-action, filled blue with white text, is BRAND intent, not "information". Left as a tone it (a) reversed to a pale blue with white text in dark mode, and (b) meant a tenant's own colour would never reach the button — on a surface that had ninety blues and exactly one brand-aware colour, which defeats the purpose of tenant theming there. The rule that resolved it is worth reusing: **a filled control the user ACTS on carries the brand; a coloured surface that INFORMS carries the tone.** Budget review time for this in every repaint sprint — "the codemod ran" is the start of the work. (Layer 1 F1, 2026-07-29)
- **Never `git checkout --` a file to undo a deliberate test-mutation: it reverts the UNCOMMITTED work in that file too.** Bite-checking a guard by injecting a fault into `globals.css` and then reverting it with `git checkout` destroyed the entire token block written minutes earlier — it was uncommitted, so HEAD was the pre-sprint file. Recoverable here only because the block was generated by a script rather than typed. **Commit first, then bite-check** — or restore by writing back a string held in the same process, never by reaching for git. (Layer 1 F1, 2026-07-29)
- **A far-future fixture date does not rot, and that is not the same as being useful.** A sandbox pool card dated 2099 rendered "Starts in 26,697 days", which a designer reads as a bug and which shows nothing about how the chip actually looks. Near-dated fixtures do rot — and when they do, the countdown simply stops rendering, which is a state another card already covers. **Prefer a fixture that degrades into a legitimate screen over one that never expires but never looks real.** A design sandbox is a design tool, not a golden master. (Layer 1 F1, 2026-07-29)


- **A backfill migration without the matching WRITE PATH is a bug with a delay on it.** Migration `0123` gave every existing sponsor a flagship membership and nothing was written to keep doing it, so the next person to register — three days later — belonged to no gift at all, and so would everyone after. The two questions are adjacent enough that answering the first ("what about the rows that already exist?") feels like answering both ("what about the row that arrives next?"), and the failure is invisible at ship time by construction: the counts reconcile, the suite passes, and the defect starts on the next INSERT, after everyone has moved on. **Whenever a migration populates a column or table for existing rows, name the code path that populates it for the next one** — if you cannot, the migration is half the change. Ten seconds to ask; nothing in our process asked it. (Sponsor gift membership, 2026-07-29)
- **A fence that hides things cannot report that it hid too much — something OUTSIDE it has to ask.** Every membership-fenced read is correct and every one fails in the same silent direction: `pool.for_sponsor` returned an empty pool, `sponsor_notifications` sent nothing, and both look identical to a legitimately empty result. That is the right direction for a privacy fence — the alternative leaks — which is precisely why the fence can never be the thing that tells you it is misconfigured. The bug surfaced only because a THIRD consumer (`record_admin_credit`) refused loudly enough to remove a button from a screen the owner happened to compare. One query — "is there an approved sponsor who can see zero students?" — would have caught it on day one. **For any deny-by-default gate, write the invariant check that runs from outside it**; the gate's own tests can only prove it excludes, never that it excluded the right set. (Sponsor gift membership, 2026-07-29)
- **`makemigrations --check` is structurally blind to production's ledger, so it cannot catch the one thing migrate-first gets wrong.** `0135_application_catalogue` had its tables created on prod with RLS and 19 seeded rows — everything its retro claimed was true — and only the `django_migrations` row was missed, leaving the ledger reading `0134`, nothing, `0136`. It would have surfaced as a `CREATE TABLE` against existing tables at the next `migrate`. The close checklist ends on `makemigrations --check`, which only confirms the FILES match the MODELS; it knows nothing about what production recorded. **When a manual step (MCP DDL) substitutes for an automated one (`migrate`), the check that guards the automated path stops guarding anything** — the substitute needs its own: compare `showmigrations` against prod at close. (Sponsor gift membership, 2026-07-29)
- **Two adjacent facts, one benign and one severe, are separated by querying production — not by reasoning from the screen.** The screenshots showed "no wallet" (normal: a wallet exists once money is recorded, and most sponsors have given nothing) sitting beside "no membership" (severe: invisible in the pool, uncreditable, silent digest). Reasoning from the surface would have sent me hunting for the missing wallet, which was never the fault. One query of the membership counts — nine sponsors with one row, one with zero — named the culprit in a single line and ruled the other out at the same time. Same family as "fix the SYSTEM, not the case": before explaining what a screen shows, ask the database what is actually true, because a screen renders the intersection of several facts and cannot tell you which one moved. (Sponsor gift membership, 2026-07-29)
- **A flag that hides the CONTROL does not gate the BEHAVIOUR — ask "what still runs when this is off?", and for anything that paints, the answer must be nothing.** Layer 1 F1 shipped the theme mechanism describing it as "behind a flag, unreachable until F7". `NEXT_PUBLIC_THEME_SWITCH` gated the switcher a person clicks. It did not gate the before-paint script in the root layout, which ran on every page and whose default mode is `auto` — *follow the device*. So from the moment F1 deployed, **every visitor whose computer is set to dark got a dark product**, across five surfaces no sprint had repainted for it. Reported from the live sponsor page by the owner; no test could have caught it, because every test ran in a light environment and the flag looked present in the code. The fix is that with the flag off there is **no script at all**, so no `data-theme` attribute exists, so the dark ramp cannot match — inert, not merely invisible. **The general test for a dark launch: name every code path that still executes with the flag off, and check that none of them can change what a user sees.** A feature ships dark when its EFFECT is unreachable, not when its button is. (Layer 1 F1 hotfix, 2026-07-29)
- **A guard that checks the SHAPE of a reference is not checking that the reference RESOLVES — and nothing notices until something dereferences it.** The Layer 0 catalogue stores an i18n key per item, and a test asserted `label_key.startswith('apply.')` with no spaces. It passed for two sprints while all nineteen keys pointed at an `apply.*` namespace **that existed in no message file**. `check-i18n.js` could not see it either: it compares the three catalogues against each other and against the source tree, and a key living in a DATABASE ROW is in neither — so the only guard that could have caught it was checking the wrong property, and the gap would have surfaced as nineteen raw dotted strings on a tenant-facing screen. Same shape as any stored reference: a storage path, a template name, a cron slug, a feature-flag name. **If a value is a pointer, the test must follow it.** Here the backend simply opens the front end's `en.json` — same repo, no guessing — which is the trick `test_subject_drift.py` already uses. (Layer 0 sprint 5 prep, TD-197, 2026-07-29)
- **When a stored reference has no target, check whether the target should be CREATED or whether one already exists under a different name.** TD-197 was written as "create the missing keys" — 57 strings across three languages. Investigation found EIGHT of the nine document labels already existed and were already translated; creating the set would have produced a second name for the same document, which is the drift the layer exists to remove. The real fix was eleven strings and a re-point. **A ticket describing a fix is a hypothesis, not an instruction** — re-derive the remedy at the moment of doing it, because the codebase has usually moved since the ticket was written.
