(This is the third time on this project a recorded remedy turned out to be wrong: TD-182 prescribed `@supabase/ssr` for an origin split it would not have fixed.) (Layer 0 sprint 5 prep, 2026-07-29)

- **A control disabled by a CONSTANT rather than a condition is a lie the UI tells, and review does not catch it.** The IC field on `/profile` had `disabled` as a bare HTML attribute with an unconditional padlock icon beside it — neither consulted `nric_verified`; the word did not appear in the block. It shipped the day the field was built and survived every review since, telling **85 of 143 production applicants** their IC was locked when nothing had locked it, including an account with no application at all. It also made Cikgu Gopal's advice ("you can correct it on your Profile page") read as nonsense to every student who followed it. Reviewers read a padlock as a *fact about the record*; nobody re-reads the JSX to check it is a fact about anything. **The tell is a control whose state SHOULD be conditional written as a constant** — and the fix is a static source guard, not a render test, because the render test proves today's wiring while the guard proves nobody goes back. (IC lock, 2026-07-29)
- **Two flags that nearly mean the same thing WILL be confused — and the broader one is the one already in scope.** `nric_verified` is the stored one-way lock; `identity_verified` is a display badge that is *also* true when the card merely matches, and is re-derived on every read. The profile page held only the second, so `disabled={identityVerified}` — the obvious one-liner — would have locked students with no genuineness check **and unlocked them again the moment they deleted the card**. What makes it nasty: for the student who MOTIVATED the sprint (typed IC disagrees with her card) the wrong flag gives the right answer, so testing the case in hand would have passed and only students who typed correctly would have been harmed. **When two predicates differ only in breadth, name the narrow one in the payload and pin the choice with a test** — "which flag" is not a detail a reader can infer. (IC lock, 2026-07-29)
- **A lock derived from evidence can be undone by deleting the evidence — so store it at the moment it is earned.** Computing "is this confirmed?" on every read is cheaper and self-healing, and here it would have been a security hole: upload a matching card → locked; delete the card → recomputes to false → unlocked → change the number → upload a different card. Anything IRREVERSIBLE must be written down when its condition first holds, never re-derived from inputs the user still controls. The general test: ask what happens if the user removes the thing your predicate reads. (IC lock, 2026-07-29)
- **Shipping a rule that fires on an EVENT changes nothing about the rows that already exist — say "zero" before quoting a number.** I told the owner 35 students would lock on deploy. The real answer was zero: the lock is taken at the end of an IC *read*, and deploying reads nothing, so those 35 would have sat open indefinitely while a student uploading the same card tomorrow locked at once. Same evidence, different answer, decided by who happened to press a button — not a rule anyone could explain to a student. Every event-triggered rule over existing data needs a sweep in the SAME change, re-applying the live predicate to the reads already stored (no re-extraction: a re-read costs money and can legitimately come back different). (IC lock, 2026-07-29)
- **Explicit staging protects OTHERS from you; it does nothing to protect you from them.** I staged explicit paths all session, per the standing rule — and a concurrent agent ran `git add -A` and swept my entire unfinished frontend into its own docs commit, then pushed it. Nothing was lost and the web build happened to be cancelled, but `main` briefly held untested UI that the next web build would have deployed. The rule as written only prevents the harm in one direction. **On a shared tree the defence is to COMMIT FREQUENTLY** — the exposure is the size of your uncommitted window, not the care of your `git add`. A finished-but-uncommitted afternoon is the thing at risk. (IC lock, 2026-07-30)
- **An approximation used to FIND something must not become the evidence FOR it.** Hunting for records whose typed IC disagreed with their card, I wrote a SQL name comparison — parentage markers stripped only between spaces, no glued-token handling — found two, and then cited those two app numbers repeatedly as proof that our reader mangles names and would wrongly accuse students. It became the stated justification for hedging the flag copy, in the retro and the CHANGELOG. Asked to look at them, the cockpit showed both **Verified · Exact**, and the real `name_match` returns `match` for both: it has a glued-token path for exactly the OCR space-split in one, and strips the trailing `A/P` in the other. The approximation was right for locating candidates and wrong for characterising them, and the real predicate was one call away in a codebase whose whole design is that the rule has ONE home. **The moment a SQL heuristic produces a finding you intend to reason from, re-run the finding through the real function.** (IC lock, 2026-07-30)

- **"Stored but never displayed" is a CLASS of defect in this codebase, not three coincidences.** In two days: the `/profile` padlock consulted nothing while 85 of 143 applicants were told their IC was locked; `qc_override_reason` recorded WHY a super accepted a case over a red flag and was absent from every payload and screen; `ai_draft_model` sat on the model, the serializer AND the TypeScript type with no render. Each was found by a human looking at a screen and asking why it said that — never by a test, because a test asserts what the code does and all three did exactly what they were written to do. The shared cause is that WRITING a field and READING it are separate acts by separate people at separate times, and nothing fails when the second never happens. **When you add a column whose purpose is to be READ BY A HUMAN — a reason, a provenance, a model name, an author — the change is not done until it is on a screen.** Worth a periodic sweep: for each such column, grep the frontend for it. (IC lock / requests deliberation, 2026-07-30)
- **A one-way conversation is a design smell you can find by ENUMERATING the operations.** The Requests module offered create · answer · triage · quote · requote · schedule · done · approve · defer · modify · decline · ai_rerun — and no way for the owner to ASK anything. The AI asked, the requester answered, and the owner was CC'd on their own request. So a judgement about the SHAPE of a request ("adding a sponsor directly bypasses the terms and consent — build an invite") had nowhere to live: `triage_note` is private and the AI never read it, so re-running the reviewer rebuilt its prompt from the same inputs and could only re-derive the same answer. The tell was visible without running anything: **list every verb a module exposes and ask who may speak to whom.** A missing verb does not appear in any failing test, any error log, or any screen — the feature simply has no shape for the thought. (Requests deliberation, 2026-07-30)
- **A guard failing when you extend a table IS the guard working — teach it, never trim it.** Adding `'ask'` to `TRANSITIONS` broke `test_refuses_from_invalid_statuses`, which derives its whole matrix from that table so a new action cannot slip in unexercised. Adding an attachment-count replacement broke a test pinning the old prompt string. Both were correct failures and both wanted a deliberate update with the reason written into the test. The instinct to reach for the assertion and soften it is exactly wrong: on the same day, `test_org_fence.py` caught an unfenced query of mine and the fix was a pragma explaining WHY, not a looser regex. **A test you edit to make green should always leave a note saying what superseded it** — otherwise the next reader cannot tell a considered change from a silenced one. (Requests deliberation, 2026-07-30)
- **On this 8 GB box the full jest run OOMs and REPORTS IT AS TEST FAILURES.** A complete run named two sponsor suites as failed, then exited 253 on a retry; `--maxWorkers=2` passed all 79 suites and 1184 tests. Running the two named suites alone also passed. So a "failure" here can be worker contention wearing the costume of a broken test, and the cheap discriminator is to re-run the named suite in isolation before believing it. Same family as the documented `next build` OOM: **treat a green/red signal from a memory-starved runner as unreliable in BOTH directions**, and use `--maxWorkers=2` for the run you intend to trust. (Requests deliberation, 2026-07-30)
- **ESLint is a DEPLOY GATE that neither `tsc` nor `jest` covers, and this repo's `tsc` noise hides that.** I verified two sprints with `npx tsc --noEmit` (grepped for touched files) and a full `npx jest`, called it green, and pushed. The web build FAILED and the api deployed without it — so the owner opened the Requests page, saw nothing had changed, and told me so. Cause: two `// eslint-disable-next-line @typescript-eslint/no-var-requires` comments I had written. The config extends only `next/core-web-vitals`, which loads no `@typescript-eslint` plugin, so **disabling a rule that does not exist is itself an error** — and `next build` runs lint before it emits. Neither of my gates runs ESLint at all. Worse, the standing practice of grepping `tsc` output for touched files exists because this repo carries ~6 pre-existing type errors in test files; that noise means a real signal in the same output is easy to wave through (I checked, and all six predated the sprint — but I had to check). **Before any push that deploys web, run `npx next lint` and require zero Errors.** Warnings are fine — the passing builds carry several. The deeper rule: the deploy gate is whatever the BUILD runs, not whatever you find convenient to run, and a half-deployed pair of services is worse than a failed pair because the half that landed makes it look shipped. (Requests deliberation / awarded sign-off, 2026-07-30)
- **`require()` inside a `describe` block was never needed and cost a deploy.** Both offending comments existed only to silence a lint rule about a `require()` I had used to pull a helper into a test — and in one of the two files the same symbol was ALREADY imported at the top of the file, so the `require()` shadowed a working import for no reason. The fix in both cases was to delete the require and use a normal top-level `import`, which removed the need for any disable comment. **When you find yourself suppressing a linter, check whether the thing being suppressed is necessary at all** — a disable comment is a claim that the rule is wrong here, and it usually is not. (Requests deliberation, 2026-07-30)
- **A plan that names ONE file is a scope claim, and mine was wrong twice in the same feature.** Paste and drag-and-drop for screenshots went into `components/OrgRequestAttachments.tsx` — which my own plan named — and the request CREATE form kept accepting uploads only, on the surface where a screenshot actually starts life (you take it, *then* you describe the bug). The owner reported it, I confirmed it shipped, and they had to report it a second time. Root cause: I searched for the attachments *component*, found exactly one, and stopped — never asking **where else does this thing enter the system?** The two surfaces cannot share code (one uploads against an existing id, the other stages files because no id exists yet), so a single component was never going to cover both, and no unit test of the shared helper could have caught it either — the helper was correct, it simply had one caller. Hours earlier I had written the lesson that a one-way conversation is found by *enumerating a module's verbs*; the same move on **surfaces** was available and I did not make it. **Before calling an input feature done, list every surface that accepts that input and check each one.** The guard is necessarily a static source check across all of them (`screenshotInput.test.ts`), because what you are asserting is that no surface was forgotten. (Requests screenshots, 2026-07-30)
- **"Commit before biting" is not ceremony — I skipped it and lost the work.** The bite-check practice says commit, then break the guard, then restore. I bit an UNCOMMITTED file, and `git checkout` restored it to the last commit, which of course did not contain the change I was validating: four edits gone, re-applied by hand. The instruction encodes exactly this failure and I read past it because the change felt small and the bite felt quick. **`git checkout` is only a restore if the thing you want back is committed** — otherwise it is a delete. On a shared tree the same reasoning already applies to `git add -A`; this is the same lesson pointed inward. (Requests screenshots, 2026-07-30)
- **A static guard that a handler is ATTACHED does not prove it can ever FIRE — and the wrong version of that assertion passed while the feature was dead.** Paste for screenshots was wired as `onPaste` on a plain `<div>` on both surfaces. A paste event is dispatched at the **focused** element and bubbles **upward**; an unfocused `<div>` with no `tabIndex` is never on that path, and neither block had a focusable child. So paste could not run anywhere, on either surface — while `screenshotInput.test.ts` asserted `/onPaste=/` and went green, and the hint text told the user the feature existed. **Shipping a promise the code cannot keep is worse than not shipping the feature**, and this was the third report from the owner on the same one. Two transferable rules. (1) **For an event that depends on focus, selection, hover or viewport, a source-shape test is not evidence** — either drive it in a real DOM, or assert the *mechanism you chose to escape the constraint* (here: `document.addEventListener('paste')`, files-only, cleaned up on unmount) and say in the test why. (2) When a fix moves a handler, **assert the old dead path is GONE** (`expect(src).not.toMatch(/onPaste=/)`) — leaving both means a paste inside the panel is handled twice and uploads the same image twice. (Requests screenshots, 2026-07-30)
- **Listening on the document is safe only if the handler bails out before `preventDefault`.** The escape from the focus problem is a document-wide `paste` listener, which by construction sees every Ctrl+V on the page — including text typed into Title or Describe. The discipline is to return early when the clipboard carries no files, and to call `preventDefault` **only after** that guard, so ordinary text paste is untouched rather than merely tolerated. Pinned by asserting the guard precedes `preventDefault` inside the handler body. **Widening a listener's scope means narrowing its trigger in the same change.** (Requests screenshots, 2026-07-30)
- **The gate that was missing already existed in this repo: 17 component tests using `@testing-library/react` + jsdom, and `CommandPalette.test.tsx` opens by stating exactly why.** Its docblock reads *"what is tested HERE is the behaviour a pure test cannot see: that it opens focused, that Enter navigates, that Escape dismisses"*. I wrote a source-shape guard for a focus-dependent feature instead, and it certified a dead one. **The rule, with a boundary that matters both ways:** a source-shape test is right for **structural** claims — no bare `disabled`, no hard-coded hex, a label only where its action is offered, no surface forgotten — because those are assertions about what the code SAYS. It is not evidence for anything depending on **focus, event propagation, keyboard, drag, mount/unmount or async flush**; that class needs rendering and dispatching. The padlock guard was the right tool; `/onPaste=/` was the wrong tool. Demonstrated rather than asserted: on the broken component, `/onPaste=/` returns **true** while the rendered test fails — so the two are not interchangeable and the cheap one can actively mislead. **Before writing a guard, ask which of the two kinds of claim you are making.** (Requests screenshots, 2026-07-30)
- **A handler is not a surface. Three attempts at one feature, each fixing the previous fix's blind spot, and the third was that nothing on screen was a target.** Paste/drag for screenshots went: (1) shipped to one of two surfaces; (2) attached to an unfocused `<div>` where it could never fire; (3) firing correctly, with **no visible drop zone** — a text link, a line of hint copy promising *"you can also paste or drag an image in"*, and a wrapper that collapsed to the height of the link when nothing was staged. The owner asked whether a surface had been built at all. It had not. Each round my verification matched the previous round's error exactly and no further: presence of a file, presence of a handler, reachability of a handler — never **"can a person see where to do this?"**. **For any input affordance, the acceptance test is a sentence about the USER, not the code: where do they aim, what tells them it is possible, and what changes when they try?** Copy that describes a capability is not the capability, and shipping the copy first is worse than shipping neither — it converts a missing feature into a broken promise, which is what made the owner test it three times. Concretely for drop zones: a real target needs padding, a dashed edge **at rest** (not only during the drag), and a state change on hover — this was the app's first one, so there was no pattern to copy and no pattern to compare against. (Requests screenshots, 2026-07-30)
- **When a stored column stands in for a HUMAN ACTIVITY, find the row where the two come apart before you build a rule on it.** The reviewers surface excluded any case whose `verdict_decided_by` was not the assignee, on the reasoning that another person's judgement should not land on a volunteer's record — and I wrote a confident docstring defending it, which made the misjudgement look considered. Production disagreed on the first row the owner checked: application #13 was assigned to Balan, he **interviewed the student and submitted his findings**, and only the final click was the owner's. I had encoded *who pressed the button* as *who did the work*. One join to `interview_sessions` would have shown the two are different questions. The general shape: a column records an EVENT, and the thing you want to say is usually about a PERSON'S CONTRIBUTION — before treating the first as the second, query for the rows where they disagree, because there are almost always some and they are exactly the cases a human will check first. (Request #10, 2026-08-02)
- **A set of bands must be written as a PARTITION, and asserted as one, or the total quietly disagrees with the parts.** The outcome bar started as two interesting cases (progressed, declined) rather than a division of all decided cases, so two applications sitting at *awaiting QC* fell into neither and Yuvarajan's page showed Completed 6 above a bar totalling 5. Nothing failed; the bar was simply short, and only arithmetic done by eye would catch it. **Whenever a UI breaks a counted total into parts, add the test that the parts sum to the total** — it costs three lines and it turns "a new status escaped the bands" from an invisible under-report into a red test. (Request #10, 2026-08-02)
- **Migrate-first is about the DATABASE; a seed command is CODE and ships with the deploy.** I wrote a handoff brief listing `seed_partner_email_templates` as a pre-push step, by analogy with the migrations above it. It runs on the deployed service and seeds whatever the RUNNING code knows about — so seeding before the deploy would have silently skipped all five new template kinds and reported success. The distinguishing question is not "is this data?" but **"where does this execute?"**: against the database (before), or inside the image (after). And when the answer matters, put the warning at the line somebody reads before firing it — the cron registry entry — not only in a document they may not open. (Request #10, 2026-08-02)
- **A stored number is not a claim about the world — and on a SHARED profile you cannot tell which it is.** Justifying a rule, I described application #15's 4.0 STPM CGPA and five STPM subjects as results she held; the owner corrected it — she sat SPM and is on a matriculation course, and those grades were typed into the COURSE GUIDE to explore programmes. The two products share one `StudentProfile` and nothing on the row records which one wrote a field, so exploration and result are indistinguishable at the database level. The decision survived (the narrow rule was right) but the REASON written into the docstring, the tests and the commit was false, and the reason is what the next reader acts on. **General rule: when a field could have been written by more than one surface, treat its PRESENCE as unproven and its ABSENCE as conclusive — build the rule on the absence, and say so where the field is read.** (BrightPath #14, 2026-08-18)
- **An absence claim about a MODEL needs a column list, not one field.** I checked `form_data` (empty on all 143 applications), concluded that nothing is snapshotted at submit, and told the owner so twice — while `intake_snapshot` sat four lines away in the same model holding a frozen copy of the profile AND the application, including the exam type whose history we were trying to reconstruct. One field's emptiness is evidence about that field. **Before saying "the system does not record X", enumerate the model's columns (`information_schema.columns` or the model file) and grep for readers — the same discipline `feedback_absence_is_a_query` applies to authored content applies to schema.** (BrightPath #14, 2026-08-18)
- **A gap between two well-tested halves is invisible to tests of either half.** Twice in one day, in the same module: `record_request_analysis`'s database path silently dropped `proposed_kind`/`proposed_lane` while its dry-run report printed them back, and `AdminOrgRequestAnswerView` passed a parameter the service had renamed, so every answer raised `TypeError` before the service and 500-ed for eighteen days. In both, the service had unit tests, the endpoint had AUTH tests, and nothing exercised the actual call between them. "Is the route covered?" was true and useless. **When a view calls a service function, one test must drive the whole path — through the URL, with the flag on and the right role — asserting what came out, not merely that the door was locked to the wrong people.** Mechanisable as a diff of view call-sites against endpoint-test URLs. (BrightPath #15 + TD-204 tooling, 2026-08-18)
- **A report that describes a value the write then discards is worse than no report** — it is checked, and it passes. `record_request_analysis --file x.json` printed `proposed : bug / small_change` and stored `('', '')`, because only the API branch passed the two kwargs. Five analyses were staged against production in that state before anyone looked. **Any dry-run that prints a field must be produced by the same code path that writes it, or the preview is a second implementation that can drift from the first.** (TD-204 tooling, 2026-08-18)
- **An "unreadable field" population is a claim about the PARSER until proven otherwise — establish WHY it cannot be read before pricing a human fallback for it.** BrightPath #12 was quoted 6.0h, ~4h of it to ask the student their SPM year, justified by four students whose slip carried no readable year. Three were being skipped by our own `exam_type` gate and the fourth by a two-character anchor (`PEPERIKSAAN` vs a clipped `PERIKSAAN`); once both were fixed the population was **zero** and the fallback took about an hour. The analysis had treated "four slips have no year" as a property of the documents when it was a property of our reading of them — on a request whose entire subject was that field. Delivered ~3.0h against 6.0h. **Before pricing "we cannot read X, so ask the human instead", read one of the X's that failed.** (BrightPath #12, 2026-08-18)
- **A derived value's home is the value it qualifies, not a banner beside it.** The SPM exam year was first designed as an always-visible note under the verification tiles — reasoning from the failure mode (the signal was quiet, so make it loud). The owner moved it onto the merit score: `94.7 (SPM 2023)`. The score is COMPUTED from the SPM grades, so the year those grades were sat is a property of that number — `94.7` and `94.7 (SPM 2023)` are not the same claim. A banner announces something *about* a number and can be scrolled past; a qualifier states what the number *is* and cannot. **When a new fact qualifies an existing displayed value, put it on that value before considering a surface of its own.** (BrightPath #12, 2026-08-18)
- **Two identical-looking failures can have OPPOSITE blast radii — check the direction before fixing them together.** The same left-edge crop on one certificate defeated two label anchors: `PEPERIKSAAN TAHUN` (the exam year) and `JUMLAH MATA PELAJARAN` (the under-read guard's declared total). Same cause, same two-character fix, and it would have been natural to do both. But the year tolerance can only **fill a blank** — wherever the full word already read, the looser pattern matches the same text at the same position, so nothing existing can change — while the count tolerance can only **reject more**: documents that parse today would start being discarded once a newly-readable declared total exceeds the recovered rows. One is measurement-free, the other owes a sweep. Shipped the first, raised **TD-217** for the second with the reason written down, so it reads as a decision rather than an omission. (BrightPath #12, 2026-08-18)
- **A support request's TITLE is a hypothesis, and the reporter is often the one who has already disproved it.** BrightPath #20 arrived as *"Kaneswaran's Reviewer report error"*; the next day Suresh withdrew that himself — *"no problem with the report !!"* — and described an entirely different problem in the same comment. Building from the title would have shipped a fix to a working report and left the real defect (a rating of the AI silently freezing a student's case) in place for another fortnight. **Read the whole thread to its last comment before believing the subject line**, and when the reporter contradicts their own title, the later text wins. (BrightPath #20, 2026-08-24)
- **A measurement wired into a decision stops being a measurement and starts being a tax on honesty.** The reviewer's four Pass/Fail marks exist to score the AI (`audit.py`: *"the 'how good is the AI' signal"*). `isClearAccept` read three of them, so a reviewer who correctly reported that our payslip reading was poor froze his own case — and the metric he was feeding punished him for feeding it truthfully. Of 63 cases that ever reached QC, **not one carries a Fail mark**; the trap caught exactly one application and there is no way to know how many reviewers learned to avoid it. **When a field's purpose is to observe, check nothing else reads it** — and prefer removing the parameter to documenting the prohibition, because a comment is a request and a signature is a rule. (BrightPath #20, 2026-08-24)
- **A branch with no message is not a quiet success, it is an invisible failure — and the fix is a total function, not one more `else`.** The reviewer pressed Save-and-approve, the submit was skipped, and the screen said nothing at all; the case sat for fourteen days with everyone believing it had moved. The repair was not a new error string but `verdictSaveOutcome`, a closed union where every value must have a line on screen, so a future branch cannot be added without one. **When you find a silent path, ask what shape would have made silence unrepresentable.** (BrightPath #20, 2026-08-24)
- **An "unset" enum value is not the absence of a slot — it is a slot of its own, and it is always empty, so whatever lands there wins by default.** A blank `household_member` looked like missing metadata. It was in fact a permanent free parking space: the slot sweep matches on `(doc_type, member, request_code)`, an untagged upload found nothing to compete with, promoted itself, and stayed live beside the good copy it should have replaced. Worse, the documents that could not be tagged were the UNREADABLE ones — so the sentinel systematically preserved the worst evidence. **Whenever a sweep, a uniqueness rule or a dedupe keys on a nullable column, ask what the null bucket does** — it is rarely "nothing". (BrightPath #20, 2026-08-24)
- **A regression test written from a production shape can still be vacuous if a DIFFERENT mechanism already produces the expected value — mutate the fix and watch it fail, every time.** The first version of the untagged-document test asserted `household_member == 'father'` and passed with the new fallback deleted: pre-consent, the STR-route force-tag already stamps every income doc, so the test was measuring the force-tag. The real case (#73) was POST-consent, which is exactly the window where the force-tag has stopped. One line — `profile_completed_at` — separated a test that guards the fix from one that guards nothing. **Deleting the fix and re-running takes thirty seconds and is the only thing that tells you which one you wrote.** (BrightPath #20, 2026-08-24)
- **Repairing a case by hand is an audit entry, so attribute it to whoever actually did it.** Application 73 was advanced to QC manually. Stamping `verified_by` with the reviewer's address would have been convenient and would have read naturally, and it would have put a false statement into a trail whose entire purpose is two-person control — the reviewer decided the case, he never pressed the button. It was stamped to the owner's account instead. **A manual repair in an audited system must look like a manual repair.** (BrightPath #20, 2026-08-24)
- **A warning that only reaches a log file has not warned anyone — and "roughly N students away" is a guess about SOMEBODY ELSE's counter.** The eWallet-ID band check logged *"widen VIRCLE_ID_BAND_MAX before the block is exhausted"* four times between 8 and 12 August; nobody reads that log, and the message named a knob that could not help once the 9-digit prefix itself rolled over. The 30 July brief had put roll-over "~55 students away" from a rate measured over one month of Vircle's whole customer base; it arrived in under two weeks (…7350 → …9897), and three students then sat with an open Vircle task and no id because a 4-digit box cannot hold 800040018xxxx. **When a limit is set by an external sequence you do not control, (a) put the exhaustion signal somewhere a human already looks (here: the relay sheet's rows with no id), and (b) make the message name the fix that will actually be needed at that point, not the one that was cheapest to write.** (eWallet ID roll-over, 2026-08-27)
- **A roadmap's file table is a hypothesis about WHERE the work lives — re-derive the render sites at sprint start.** Layer 0 Sprint 4's approved plan named `apply/page.tsx` + `buildApplicationPayload` as the scope for the question switches; every governed question actually renders on the Step-4 wizard (`ScholarshipNextSteps.tsx`), and the apply page needed no change at all. The table was written four weeks earlier, BEFORE Sprint 3b's own discovery that the JSX is what governs what a student sees — so the plan encoded a superseded belief about its own codebase. A five-minute grep of the catalogue codes against the components found the real sites; coding to the table would have wired governance into a page whose only catalogue question is optional and never gates, and missed the wizard entirely. (Layer 0 Sprint 4, 2026-08-30)
- **When a completeness part becomes conditional, pair every "it stopped gating" assertion with "its neighbour still gates".** Two story questions switched off and ALL questions accidentally off produce identical results in a test that only checks the two — the same one-sided-assertion trap as the payload tests' "assert the other bill did NOT move". The vacuous value must be True and the proof it didn't over-loosen lives entirely in the untouched neighbour. (Layer 0 Sprint 4, 2026-08-30)
- **Pin a prompt-level guarantee the moment configuration makes its input state ROUTINE.** "A blank field renders 'not provided' and the model is told to say nothing about it" had never needed a test while blank meant a student skipped an optional box; the questions catalogue makes blank-by-configuration a normal state, so an unpinned rewording of either half (the sentinel or the instruction) would quietly turn every switched-off question into "a student who ignored the form" in sponsor profiles. One builder test per half. (Layer 0 Sprint 4, 2026-08-30)
- **Put a freeze at the SEAM every consumer already reads, not at each consumer.** The submit-time snapshot is read inside `requirements.resolve`, so the completeness gate, the payload, the verdict facts and the ticket queue all honour it with zero edits — and a future consumer cannot forget to. Wiring the frozen copy into `application_completeness` alone (the obvious place, where the bug bit) would have left the payload drawing the live form while the gate enforced the frozen one: the same two-descriptions drift Layer 0 exists to remove, reintroduced by its own safety net. (Layer 0 snapshot, 2026-08-30)
- **A plan's SEQUENCE decays the same way its file table does — quote it, do not restate it.** Five sprints into the themes arc I wrote "NEXT = F6" in a close-out block, and the approved roadmap says `F1 → F2a → F2b → F3 → F4 → F5 → A1 → A2 → A3 → F6 → F7`. The order had a stated reason (repaint every surface a tenant sees BEFORE giving them a colour editor, so their first colour lands coherently) and I had simply stopped re-reading it. The owner caught it. The error had already propagated into the Next Sprint block, the project memory and the dashboard — three places a future session would have trusted. **The file table gets re-derived every sprint because a lesson told me to; the sequence needs exactly the same treatment. Paste the roadmap's sequence line verbatim into the close-out rather than paraphrasing it from memory.** (Layer 1 F5 close, caught 2026-09-01)
- **A JSX comment is only valid in CHILDREN position — three variants of that mistake in one session.** `{/* … */}` between an element's attributes, inside a `cond && (` expression, and in a ternary are all parse errors; only children position works. The pull is always the same: the explanation wants to sit next to the thing it explains, and the thing it explains is usually an attribute or a condition. Put it ABOVE the element, or use a `//` comment inside a `${…}` block. Knowing this once did not stop it recurring, because the mistake is made while thinking about the reasoning, not the syntax. (Layer 0 Sprint 5, Layer 1 F2b, Layer 1 F5, 2026-09-01)
- **AGE predicts hidden colour far better than SIZE does.** The officer cockpit is the biggest file in the product — 3,490 lines, 537 colour utilities — and its hiding-place hunt came back completely clean: no hex, no arbitrary-value classes, no inline styles, no entities. The files that hid colour were the old, accreted ones: `profile`, `onboarding`, the error and loading shells, `globals.css` itself. A file written recently and in one idiom tends to be uniform; a file that grew over years has strata. **When budgeting a migration, weight the estimate by how long a file has existed, not by how large it is.** (Layer 1 F5, 2026-09-01)
- **NEVER GENERATE A REGEX. A guard written by a script can be born dead, and it reports success.** F4's filled-control check was produced by a Python heredoc, where a backslash followed by b is not two characters but a literal BACKSPACE byte (0x08). The regex compiled cleanly as "backspace, then text-white", matched nothing in any file, and passed on every run. It was found ONLY by bite-checking: three of four injected faults failed loudly and the fourth produced no failure at all, and chasing that silence took fifteen minutes because the file list was right and the logic was right in isolation — only reading the line's BYTES explained it. The sibling bug had already appeared in the same session (F3b, `\s` inside a template literal collapsing to `s`) and I still reached for a generator. **Code that writes code has its escapes eaten twice, and the failure is a dead test rather than a broken build — which is strictly worse.** Write regexes by hand; if a file must be generated, sweep it for control characters afterwards. (Layer 1 F4, 2026-09-01)
- **Before concluding a guard is broken, prove the fault actually landed.** Half of the confusion above was a second injection that silently did nothing: a `replace` that hit one of four references, leaving the set still valid, so the guard was right to stay quiet. A bite-check has two halves — inject, then VERIFY THE INJECTION — and skipping the second turns a passing guard into a suspect. (Layer 1 F4, 2026-09-01)
- **Three copies of a mapping and a comment asking them to agree is not a mechanism.** The role→colour palette was declared in `StaffAdmin`, the reviewers list and the Manual, the last carrying the note *"Reuse the Administration panel's role-badge palette so a role reads the same colour everywhere."* A codemod converted one copy and left the others, and the same role would have rendered one colour in Administration and another in the Manual — a difference a reader would blame on the data. **When a comment asks two files to stay in step, that is a request for a shared module, and it should be read as a bug report.** (Layer 1 F4, 2026-09-01)
- **A guard that forbids a whole family also forbids the fix — state the RULE, not a ban.** "A theme may never write `--brand-*`" enforced the owner's ruling by banning the family outright. The ruling was about IDENTITY: a mode change must not alter whose product you are looking at. Banning all ten stops also banned re-aiming the nine DERIVED ones at a dark page, which is why 101 pale-brand panels across 40 files had no legal fix. Restating it as "`--brand-500` is byte-identical across modes" made the guard STRICTER — nothing had actually checked the identity stop before — while making the repair possible. **When a guard blocks work that seems obviously right, re-read what it was protecting; a ban is a proxy for a property, and the property is usually both narrower and more testable.** (Layer 1 F3b, 2026-08-31)
- **Write a test from what the code says, not from what the change intends.** Two of my new brand assertions failed on entirely correct values: "a brand tint is lighter than the page" (in light mode a 95%-white blue is very slightly DARKER than a `#f9fafb` page) and "the light ramp is generated by `brandRamp()`" (it is deliberately the platform's seeded hexes — the function's own docstring says so, and I had read it an hour earlier). Failing on correct code is the cheap direction, but such a test is one impatient edit from deletion, after which it guards nothing. (Layer 1 F3b, 2026-08-31)
- **`\s` inside a TEMPLATE LITERAL is not `\s`.** `` new RegExp(`--${name}:\s*(...)`) `` compiles `\s` as an unknown escape and collapses it to a bare `s`, so the regex matches nothing and every assertion using it throws. Three other copies of the same helper in the same file had the doubled backslash and were fine, which is precisely why the broken one read as correct. In a template literal a regex needs a DOUBLED backslash — `backslash backslash s` — so that what reaches `RegExp` is the single-escaped form. (Written out in words on purpose: the first draft of this very lesson lost its own backslash to the script that wrote the file, and ended up asserting that the broken form was the fix.) (Layer 1 F3b, 2026-08-31)
- **A colour can hide in a CLASS NAME that is not a colour name.** `bg-[#f8fafc]` is Tailwind's arbitrary-value syntax: it sets the entire page ground and it passes every guard that looks for `bg-gray-50`, because it IS a class and it simply is not on the list. Six student pages — including the error and loading screens — were still painting themselves near-white this way, three sprints into a migration whose whole point was to remove exactly that. The scan has to match the SHAPE of a colour (`-[#…]`), not an enumeration of known colour names. The running list of hiding places is now: inline styles and gradients (F1), the stylesheet's own `@layer` rules and the browser's default for an undeclared control (F2a), lookup tables returning class strings (F2b), arbitrary-value classes and raw hex in SVG props (F3). **Assume there is one more.** (Layer 1 F3, 2026-08-31)
- **Before inventing a new token, check whether the ramp's STOPS already say it.** A progress set needed two "good" states a student could tell apart — still on track, and graduated — and the vocabulary has one `positive`. The obvious moves were a fifth tone (a lie: graduating is not a different KIND of good) or a category swatch (worse: it is a state, and a tenant's arbitrary colour must never be what says "you made it"). Weight settled it: `on_track` stays a light tint, `graduated` becomes the filled chip. Same tone, more emphasis, on the thing that actually finished. **A ramp has stops, and emphasis is a legitimate axis — reach for it before widening the vocabulary.** (Layer 1 F3, 2026-08-31)
- **Counting what a SET needs finds bugs that reading the code never will.** Converting the category palettes meant asking "how many distinct colours does this set require?" — six institution types, seven entry conditions. The answer exposed two collisions that predated every line of theming work: `ua` and `pismp` were both purple, and `noColorblind` and `noDisability` were both red. Nobody had noticed, because each renders alone on the page where it appears; you only see it by putting the set beside itself. **When a lookup table's contract is "these must differ", assert the CARDINALITY of the distinct values, not the values** — that guard finds pre-existing breakage on the day you write it. (Layer 1 F2c, 2026-08-31)
- **A note written to argue for a decision goes stale the moment the decision is made.** The sandbox surface built to show the owner a gap still said "they DO NOT follow dark mode — that is the gap" after the gap was closed, sitting above a screenshot proving otherwise. Advocacy text is documentation with an expiry date. **Whichever sprint acts on the decision owns rewriting the case that was made for it.** (Layer 1 F2c, 2026-08-31)
- **A rename that is correct per-item can still be destructive per-SET — before any bulk rename, ask which of the old values were required to be DIFFERENT from each other.** Layer 1's codemod maps colour families onto four tones, which is right for a signal and silently lossy for a set of categories: `poly` (emerald) and `ILJTM` (green) would both have become `positive`, so two institution types a student uses to compare courses would render identically, and `sains_komputer` (blue) and `sains_sosial` (sky) would have collapsed the same way. Every individual rename was defensible; the SET lost information. Nothing would have failed — not a test, not a type, not the eye, because a page full of consistent colours looks correct. **The tell is a lookup table whose values are presentation: `Record<category, colour>`. Its contract is mutual distinctness, and no family-level transform can preserve that.** Where the target vocabulary has no name for what the set means, exempt it and guard the exemption rather than forcing the nearest fit. (Layer 1 F2b, 2026-08-31)
- **A FALLING error count on a codebase with known debt means look, not celebrate.** `tsc --noEmit` had reported 24 pre-existing errors for two sprints; it reported 3, and for a moment that read as progress. A syntax error had made the compiler stop early. Any gate whose output is a number needs an EXPECTED value, not a direction — "expect exactly 24" would have failed instantly where "expect fewer than before" applauded. (Layer 1 F2b, 2026-08-31)
- **A JSX comment may not sit between an element's attributes.** `<Link href="…" {/* why */} className="…">` is a parse error, and it is the natural place to want to explain an attribute, which is why knowing it once did not stop me doing it three more times a sprint later. The comment goes ABOVE the element. Cheap to catch (`tsc` and `next lint` both name the line) and cheap to avoid. (Layer 0 Sprint 5 and Layer 1 F2b, 2026-08-31)
- **A derivation that preserves VALUES can still destroy ROLES — check what each step is FOR, not what colour it became.** Layer 1's dark set is the light set read from the other end, which is right for the four tones (a signal only has to stay legible reversed) and wrong for the ground. In light the raised surface is white: the EXTREME of the ramp. Reverse it and the card lands at the other extreme — pure black, DARKER than its own `#111827` page — so every card, input and modal read as a hole punched through the page instead of an object resting on it. Nothing was miscoloured; each stop held exactly the value the rule promised. **When a ramp's positions carry meaning (raised / page / well / border), pin the RELATIONSHIP as the test — "the raised surface is lighter than its page, in both modes" — never the formula that happened to produce it**, so a later tuning pass can pick any numbers and still cannot reintroduce the bug. (Layer 1 F2a, 2026-08-31)
- **The stylesheet and the browser's own defaults are hiding places for colour, and the second one has no line of code to find.** Every conversion guard reads `.tsx`, so `globals.css` belonged to no sprint's file list: `body` sat at `bg-white text-gray-900` (a WHITE page in dark mode, product-wide) and `.input` declared no background at all. The `.input` case is the instructive one — nothing was wrong with the markup, because the white was coming from the user agent, so no scan of any kind could have reported it. It is invisible in light mode precisely because the UA default and the page agree. **Add to the "ways the old thing can be spelled" list: the stylesheet's own `@layer` blocks, and every control that inherits a colour rather than declaring one.** (Layer 1 F2a, 2026-08-31)
- **A guard that greps source text will flag its own documentation — strip comments before every scan.** The hex guard reported `#15a`/`#15b` (audit references in comments; three valid hex digits) and then `#fff`, from the note explaining why `#fff` had just been removed; the stylesheet guard then reported `bg-white`/`text-gray-900` from the note explaining why `body` no longer says them. The most likely place in a codebase to *write* a forbidden string is the sentence explaining why it is forbidden. Without a comment-stripping pass the only way to pass the guard is to stop explaining yourself, which is the opposite of what it is for. (Layer 1 F2a, 2026-08-31)
- **A data loader never depends on a translator or formatter handle — translate at render.** The programme-configuration page's `load` was a `useCallback` over `t` because it translated its error text; with a `t` that is a fresh function each render (the test's mock — and any hook is one refactor from that), the effect re-fired on every render and each fetch's `setConfig` silently wiped the unsaved draft. Store the error as a flag or code, translate where it is drawn, and keep the loader's dependencies to the token and the gate. The rendered jsdom test of the REAL page caught it; the pure-helper tests never could. (Layer 0 Sprint 5, 2026-08-30)
- **A fresh web worktree has no `node_modules`, and `npx` will quietly run something else.** `npx jest` started installing jest 30 from the network and then failed on `ts-jest`; `npx tsc` printed a package's "not the tsc you are looking for" banner. Link or install `node_modules` in the worktree BEFORE trusting any gate (a directory junction to the main checkout's install is enough), and treat a gate that prints anything unfamiliar as not having run. (Layer 0 Sprint 5, 2026-08-30)
- **A module-scope `process.env` read is a TESTABILITY decision, not just a performance one.** `branding-context` read `NEXT_PUBLIC_ORG_CODE` once at module scope, which is idiomatic and, in Next, free — the value is inlined at build time either way. It also meant the only way to mount the provider as a different tenant was `jest.resetModules()` plus a fresh import, and that hands the freshly-imported component a SECOND copy of React whose hook dispatcher is null: every case died on `useState`, and the failure names React rather than the env read. Moving the read into a one-line function made seven jsdom cases possible and changed nothing in production. **When a module-scope constant is derived from the environment, ask what a test would have to do to vary it; if the answer is "re-import the module", read it lazily instead.** (Layer 1 A1, 2026-09-01)
- **When one calculation exists in two languages, make BOTH sides assert the same fixture — and hand-verify the fixture rather than generating it from either.** A1's ramp lives in Python (save time) and TypeScript (the un-themed fallback). A drift would not throw; a tenant's colours would simply shift by one channel the moment a theme row was created, which nobody would read as a bug. A shared golden makes the drift fail loudly in whichever language moved. It earned its place immediately: **Python's `round()` is banker's rounding and JavaScript's `Math.round` is not** — `round(100.5)` is 100 in Python and 101 in JS — and the chosen colour lands on an exact `.5`. Taking the fixture FROM the implementation would have pinned the disagreement instead of catching it. (Layer 1 A1, 2026-09-01)
- **A guard that is redundant today can be the whole guard tomorrow — write it against the property, and test it without the thing that makes it redundant.** The theme fence refuses a platform tone AND refuses anything outside the tenant allow-list. Today the second subsumes the first, so the tone check looks like dead code. But the allow-list is scheduled to widen (A4 adds a ground family), and an allow-list-only guard would weaken by exactly the width of that change, silently, in a sprint that has no reason to think about tones. The tone test therefore asserts the refusal per family and never mentions the allow-list. **The tell is a guard whose sufficiency depends on a constant somebody is already planning to edit.** (Layer 1 A1, 2026-09-01)
- **`status.traffic[0]` is NOT the live Cloud Run revision, and the wrong answer looks completely plausible.** Verifying the A1 deploy, `gcloud run services describe halatuju-web --format='value(status.traffic[0].revisionName)'` returned `halatuju-web-00094-ful` — a revision from roughly seven hundred deploys ago. The traffic list is not ordered by share: this service carries a TAGGED revision (`stpm`, for a preview URL) at index zero, and the serving one at index one. Nothing errors, and `…-00094-ful` reads like a revision name, so the only tell is that the number is implausibly low — which you notice only if you happen to remember the last one. **Read `status.latestReadyRevisionName`, or filter the traffic list to the entry whose `percent` is 100.** The general shape: when a tool returns a LIST and you want "the current one", find the field that says current; positional access is a guess that will be right until someone adds a tag. (Layer 1 A1 deploy, 2026-09-01)
- **When a codemod's unit is a CLASS STRING but the property's unit is a RENDERED ELEMENT, the residue must be read, not counted.** A2's sweep moved every filled control off the too-light brand stop by matching class strings containing both the background and `text-white`. It reported 17 survivors as shapes. Three were not: the landing page's stats band sets the background on a `<section>` and `text-white` on its child grid, and the course card sets it on a wrapper with the number in a child `<span>`. CSS composes those pairs across two elements and the codemod cannot see it — while the count looks authoritative, which is what makes it convincing. **Reading all 17 took two minutes and found two live text-on-too-pale surfaces, one of them on the public landing page.** The general shape: a mechanical sweep is trustworthy exactly to the width of its unit, so the residue is where the judgement has to happen. (Layer 1 A2, 2026-09-01)
- **A gate that refuses your own product is reporting a defect in the product — check that before you touch the threshold.** A2's contrast gate, written against the pairs the app really renders, refused the platform's own live brand colour. The reflex is to conclude the gate is too strict and loosen it. The honest question is *which pair, and is the PRODUCT wrong there?* — and it was: 54 filled controls were on a brand stop that F4 had already ruled wrong for them, putting small white text at 3.98 against a bar of 4.5. Fixing the product made the gate both honest AND less restrictive: our colour passed, and orange became usable. **The test that catches this is now named as a canary and its docstring asks the question, because the loosening instinct is strong and arrives at exactly the moment nobody wants more work.** (Layer 1 A2, 2026-09-01)
- **A falling `tsc --noEmit` count is a parse error, not an improvement.** 24 → 6 looked like somebody had fixed things; it meant TypeScript had stopped parsing a file and therefore stopped reporting the pre-existing errors inside it. The cause was a JSX comment placed inside a `cond && (` expression AND containing braces — two faults in one line, both variants of a lesson already written twice. **Meanwhile jest passed 1,573 tests over that unparseable file**, because jest does not type-check and nothing rendered the component. The standing rule *"expect exactly 24, never fewer than before"* is now three-for-three, and the asymmetry is worth naming: a RISING count is a new error you will go and read; a FALLING one is a silence you have to be suspicious of on purpose. (Layer 1 A2, 2026-09-01)
- **A `first:` or `last:` variant is scoped to the element's OWN parent — on an only child it matches EVERYTHING, silently.** A2's ten-step palette put `first:rounded-l-md` on each colour block to round the left end of the strip. Each block is the only child of its own grid cell, so every one of the ten matched, and the ramp rendered as ten separate chips with no rounded right end at all. It compiles, it looks deliberate, and no test noticed because a test asserts there are ten cells, not that they read as one object. **Found by the owner opening the page.** The rule: when a variant means "the first of a SET", check that the element's parent actually is that set — in a grid of wrapper cells it never is, and the honest tool is the index. (Layer 1 A2, 2026-09-01)
- **A test that borrows the wrong client asks the wrong question, and can pass while doing it.** A3's most important test — "a draft never reaches a visitor" — called the PUBLIC branding endpoint using `self.client`, which was carrying the org_admin's token. It failed with a confusing `KeyError` because the NRIC gate refused the request, and the confusion is the lucky part: had the response happened to be a valid payload, the test would have gone green while measuring what an ADMINISTRATOR sees, which is a different question with a different correct answer. **When a test's name names a person — a visitor, a student, an anonymous browser — the request has to look like that person's, and reusing the suite's authenticated client is the easiest way to quietly stop doing so.** Build the unauthenticated client explicitly and say why in a comment. (Layer 1 A3, 2026-09-01)
- **When a new field's default changes what an EXISTING row means, every direct constructor in the suite is in the blast radius — including tests nobody edited.** A3 gave themes a `status` defaulting to `draft`. Three `test_branding_endpoint` tests build rows directly and assert they are served; after the change those rows were drafts, so they correctly stopped being served and failed. Nothing about those tests was wrong before and nothing in them changed — the MEANING of what they construct did. The same reasoning applies to the migration: a production row that predated the column was the live theme, so the migration promotes it rather than letting a default silently un-publish a tenant. **Ask of any new field with a default: what does it now claim about rows that already exist, and about every row a test builds by hand?** (Layer 1 A3, 2026-09-01)
- **A plan names a surface as well as a file count, and BOTH age — re-derive the surface too.** F6 was written down as *"the public course guide, 36 files"*. The count was right to the file and the description was not: what was actually left was the course guide PLUS contracts, the email editor, sources, reviewers, the interview panel and the staff manual. Reading the title narrowly would have shipped a sprint that left F7 blocked by files nobody had scheduled, and the sprint would have looked complete. The standing rule (re-derive the file TABLE at sprint start) already existed and I followed it — one grep for unconverted files — which is the only reason the mismatch surfaced at all, because that grep answers both questions in one pass. **When a plan describes WHERE the work is, treat the description with the same suspicion as the count.** (Layer 1 F6, 2026-09-02)
- **When a lookup table covers only PART of its domain, the colour is asserting something false about the members it omits — and the fallback branch tells you how big the omission is.** The institution card tinted six of Malaysia's sixteen states and greyed the other ten. It survived four repaint sprints because it read as decoration, and it only became undeniable when the codemod renamed four of the six onto TONES: Kuala Lumpur became `critical` and Johor `positive`. Reading that output surfaced the real question, which was never *which token* but **what is this colour claiming** — a distinction it abandoned after six members, about a fact already written on the card in words. The tell is cheap to check and I have never seen it on a checklist: **count the domain, count the keys, and read the `|| fallback` as the size of what the palette is silently declining to say.** (Layer 1 F6, 2026-09-02)
- **A guard scoped to a file LIST is blind by construction to whatever is not on the list — which is exactly where the next defect will be.** F3 discovered arbitrary-value colour classes (`bg-[#f8fafc]` sets a whole page ground and passes any scan that enumerates colour NAMES), wrote the guard, and scoped it to `F3_FILES`. F6 found the identical class in two quiz pages, three sprints later, because no sprint's list had ever covered them. Per-surface scoping was RIGHT while the migration was partway through — the raw families had to stay legal somewhere — so the lesson is not "always scan everything"; it is that **a list-scoped guard carries a debt that comes due, and the sprint that removes the last exemption owes the widening.** Running list of hiding places: inline styles/gradients (F1), the stylesheet's own layers and an undeclared control (F2a), lookup tables of class strings (F2b), arbitrary-value classes and SVG props (F3), the same arbitrary-value class in unlisted files (F6). **Assume there is one more.** (Layer 1 F6, 2026-09-02)
- **A lesson applied to ONE call site is a lesson half-applied.** F2a established that a guard reading source text must strip comments, or the only way to pass it is to stop explaining yourself — and fixed the hex scan. F6 tripped the same wire twice in one afternoon: the raw-colour scan accused this sprint's own note explaining why a tint was removed (it has to name `bg-gray-50` to explain it), and the white-literal check accused `contrast.ts`, whose comment states the very invariant it enforces and therefore quotes both sides. The scan that had been fixed was silent; its two neighbours, which do the same kind of reading, were not. Same shape as this codebase's August billing lesson — *"wrapped the N call sites that exist"* — pointed at a test file. **When you fix a class of bug in one function, grep for the other functions that do the same kind of work, in the same file, before closing it.** (Layer 1 F6, 2026-09-02)
- **A vocabulary that must stay MUTUALLY distinguishable has a hard ceiling, and the honest move at the ceiling is to stop colouring, not to widen.** Two F6 sets did not fit: seventeen STPM subject codes wanted sixteen hues, and the type + level chips sit adjacent on one card and wanted thirteen between them. The family has eight, and the eight deliberately avoid green/blue/amber/red so a category is never mistaken for a status. Sixteen hues that dodge those four, separate from each OTHER, and survive the light→dark role swap do not exist — widening would have produced exactly the indistinguishability the family was created to prevent, at twice the scale. Both went neutral and neither lost anything, because in both cases the chip already renders its own name beside it (`BIO  Biology`, "Diploma") and the level chip had ALWAYS been grey when unrecognised. **Before widening a categorical palette, ask what the colour is telling a reader that the label does not** — and check the ceiling against the constraint the family was built around, not against the number of members. (Layer 1 F6, 2026-09-02)
- **The screen that puts a SET beside itself for the first time is where its distinctness is actually tested — and a guard asserting distinct VALUES cannot stand in for it.** F6's sandbox surface is the first in the product's history to render all eight `category-N` swatches at once; every prior screen showed one badge at a time. Measured in dark, `Politeknik rgb(19, 78, 74)` and `Kolej Komuniti rgb(22, 78, 99)` are distinct by value — the guard passes, correctly — and hard to separate by eye. The guard is not wrong; "distinct values" is what a test can check, and "tellable apart by a person" is what a design surface is for. F2c built the family, F2c's guard proved the numbers differ, and nobody had yet SEEN the set. **When a palette's contract is mutual distinctness, the acceptance step is a screen showing every member together — schedule it in the sprint that creates the family, not three sprints later.** (Layer 1 F6, 2026-09-02)
- **A fixture that renders a legitimate-looking screen is part of the deliverable, because a design surface is READ rather than asserted.** The first `merit_label` in F6's new sandbox fixture was `'Good'` — outside the closed set (`High | Fair | Low`) — so it fell through to "Low Chance" with a red bar on all eight cards. A reviewer opening that screen sees eight alarming cards and starts debugging the thing they were asked to look at, which is the opposite of what the surface is for. Same family as F1's far-future-date lesson, and the fix costs nothing: cycling the three labels also shows the merit tones honestly. **Before handing someone a review surface, look at it as its reader and ask what it appears to be claiming.** (Layer 1 F6, 2026-09-02)
